[PMID: 42708800] Intravenous lidocaine reduces the propofol EC50 for loss of consciousness and intraoperative anesthetic consumption in gynecological laparoscopy: A randomized controlled trial.

<h4>Background</h4>Intravenous lidocaine reduces propofol requirements and procedure-related adverse events.<h4>Objectives</h4>The study aimed to test whether intravenous lidocaine would reduce the effect-site concentration of propofol required to achieve loss of consciousness and decrease propofol consumption during total intravenous anesthesia in gynecological laparoscopy.<h4>Methods</h4>This was a prospective, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial. Sixty patients were randomly allocated to receive either intravenous lidocaine (1.5 mg·kg-¹ bolus) followed by continuous infusion or an equal volume of saline. Propofol was administered via target-controlled infusion starting at an effect-site concentration of 3.5 μg/mL. The concentration was then adjusted in steps of 0.5 μg/mLaccording to Dixon's up-and-down sequential method: decreased if loss of consciousness was achieved, or increased if not. Loss of consciousness was defined as loss of response to verbal commands. The median effective concentration (EC50) of propofol for inducing loss of consciousness was calculated using the Dixon's up-and-down method. General anesthesia was maintained with propofol and remifentanil, guided by state entropy (target 40-60) and surgical pleth index (target 20-50). Drug consumption was normalized to anesthesia duration and body weight.<h4>Results</h4>The estimated EC50 of propofol for inducing loss of consciousness was significantly lower in the lidocaine group than in the saline group (3.32 μg/mL, 95% Confidence Interval (CI): 3.04-3.59 vs. 3.89 μg/mL, 95% CI: 3.50-4.28). Under the study protocol, the lidocaine group also required less propofol (8.62 mg·kg-1·h-1, 95% CI: 8.10-9.15 vs. 9.89 mg·kg-1·h-1, 95% CI: 9.05-10.73) and less remifentanil (0.23 μg·kg-1·min-1, 95% CI: 0.21-0.24 vs. 0.27 μg·kg-1·min-1, 95% CI: 0.24-0.30) compared with the saline group.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Intravenous lidocaine reduced the propofol EC50 for Loss of Consciousness (LOC) and decreased intraoperative propofol and remifentanil consumptions in patients undergoing gynecological laparoscopy. These findings suggest a propofol- and opioid-sparing effect of intravenous lidocaine in this setting, although confirmation in larger multicenter trials is needed.

[PMID: 42844819] Protecting the Privacy of Patients With Impaired Consciousness in Intensive Care: A Qualitative Study.

<h4>Background</h4>Maintaining privacy in intensive care units (ICUs) is challenging because of high workload, invasive procedures, continuous monitoring and shared care environments. Patients with impaired consciousness may be particularly vulnerable because their ability to express privacy preferences, assert personal boundaries and advocate for themselves is limited.<h4>Aim</h4>To explore intensive care nurses' experiences of protecting privacy when caring for patients with impaired consciousness.<h4>Study design</h4>A descriptive qualitative study was conducted with 20 intensive care nurses in Türkiye between March and April 2026. Data were collected through semi-structured online interviews and analysed using inductive thematic analysis. The study was reported in accordance with the Consolidated Criteria for Reporting Qualitative Research guidelines.<h4>Findings</h4>Four themes were identified: the marginalisation of privacy under clinical priorities; structural and process-related constraints on privacy in intensive care; loss of subjectivity and the mechanisation of care in patients with impaired consciousness; and ethical and psychosocial implications of privacy violations.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Privacy in intensive care is shaped not only by individual ethical awareness but also by workload, environmental conditions, care routines and communication practices. Patients with impaired consciousness have the same rights to privacy and dignity as other patients; however, their reduced ability to express preferences, assert personal boundaries and advocate for themselves may make privacy violations less visible and increase their dependence on nurses for protection.<h4>Relevance to clinical practice</h4>Protecting the privacy of patients with impaired consciousness in ICUs requires structured care routines, standardised bedside communication, minimisation of unnecessary exposure during procedures and targeted ethical awareness training to support dignity in everyday practice.

[PMID: ] How Can Consciousness Propagate Through Chemical Synapses? Conscious Ontogenetic Development Through Phasic Release and Synaptic Density, Within Subconscious Priors of Tonic Stimulation

The transmission of consciousness through a chemical synapses is an under-specified probable occurrence. Chemical transmission is essential for neuronal propagation and neural propagation is essential within many existing measurements of consciousness. The development and plasticity of consciousness throughout an animal's lifetime requires grounded chemical distinctions, executive and non-executive processing and discrete neurotransmission should be neurobiologically consistent with existing accounts of consciousness. These hypotheses propose chemical-conscious correlates between neurons and are attached through evolution to established cognitive and environmental mechanisms. These hypotheses suggest consciousness may be chemically affected at global scales and developed ontogenetically during continuous environmental rationalization and all subsequent propagation. Single-synaptic phasic transmitter transients are positioned as executive signals within neural propagation, selecting synaptic and possibly epigenetic reconsolidation while directing attention towards significance within tonic stimulation. This framing is consistent with phasic and tonic dynamics, with each transmitter being differentiated by their established functions. Phasic transients are further suggested to direct cortical spreading (an established signature of consciousness) under tonic, epigenetic, and synaptic allowance. Because phasic release fundamentally affects synaptic plasticity, while synapses determine the threshold and magnitude of phasic response, this framing allows a developmental and individually variable model of consciousness. Existing theories may be extended under this hypothetical developmental framing, such as Predictive Processing, Global Neuronal Workspace Theory (GNWT), Integrated Information Theory (IIT), and Affective Neuroscience; extending each framework by approaching phasic response as a significance signal at the synaptic scale, providing mechanisms through which present conscious experience updates future conscious experience. Because each hypothesis is falsifiable, provided predictions and experimental directions can largely determine the accuracy of each claim; given hypotheses are precise enough to fail and many of their correlating experiments are practical enough to proceed. If this account holds, it points toward practical improvements in consciousness research, chemical synaptic mechanisms, depression treatments, therapeutic approaches, memory rehabilitation, learning environment refinement strategies, and early developmental interventions.

[PMID: ] Astrocytes as a Bridging Mechanism: From Circuits to Cortex in Consciousness

This article reviews the currently reported coupling properties of astrocytes in neural circuits, as well as the potentially promising functional structures and molecular mechanisms associated with astrocytic specialized domains and the spatiotemporal specificity of calcium levels. It proposes a model of astrocytes as a bridging mechanism—from microcircuit specialization to the functional connectivity of brain regions and nuclei—that addresses what signals are involved, how integration is achieved, and how these processes act on long-term memory and the “brain-body” characteristics of consciousness. The model posits that, in the formation of consciousness and cognition, astrocytes and their functional structures bridge the neuronal encoding of multimodal embodied perception, integrate and transmit it, and generate delayed, low-frequency calcium waves corresponding to the current state of consciousness or cognition. Through a relatively global signal, they direct the dynamics of the cortical connectome, thereby acting, at the level of brain dynamics, as a downstream bridging router for memory and shaping the inertial circuits of consciousness over long time domains. At the microenvironmental level, astrocytes and their subtypes in different regions shape neural circuits through metabolic coupling based on layer-specific spatial transcriptomes, reversibly shaping the cellular and synaptic basis of long-term memory. By virtue of their broad receptor repertoire expression, astrocytes serve as terminal receptors for perceptual information. At the circuit level, through syncytium-based global calcium waves, they form a global signal, thereby locally and specifically influencing and globally regulating cortical activity and coordinating the cortical connectivity and spatial transcription of consciousness. In terms of embodied consciousness, astrocytes bridge, through neurovascular coupling and global signals, the bidirectional communication between consciousness as “brain-body synchrony” and bodily rhythms such as blood flow and respiration.

[PMID: 42775147] Pluralism within limits: how to make the idea of multiple NCC kinds useful.

Recently, several authors have argued that the current impasse in consciousness science supports a pluralistic interpretation: the view that seemingly contradictory findings may both be valid because consciousness can be brought about in different ways, each sufficient to generate conscious experience. We analyze different versions of pluralism to determine under what conditions it offers a viable interpretation of consciousness science. We argue that viable pluralism requires a two-factor framework distinguishing general consciousness factors (mechanisms rendering contents conscious) from content encoding itself. Moreover, neural correlates of consciousness (NCC) kinds must be finite in number and systematically ordered to enable a unified scientific model. While pluralism enables testable hypotheses, significant ontological tensions arise: how can consciousness constitute a unified natural kind if realized by fundamentally distinct NCC kinds? Drawing on philosophy of science, we propose an alternative interpretation in terms of scientific rather than natural kinds. Our goal is not to advocate for pluralism, but rather, to critically examine its viability and implications. Based on this, we urge the field to engage more explicitly with the foundational assumptions that shape consciousness research.

[PMID: ] Spontaneous slow waves index multiple dimensions of recovery in Disorders of Consciousness

<h4>ABSTRACT</h4>  Whether and how residual awareness is expressed in patients with Disorders of Consciousness (DoC) depends on the severity of brain damage and the transient functional state of the cortex at assessment. Classically, DoC are associated with electroencephalographic (EEG) slowing, marked by high-amplitude slow waves, even during the day. Yet, slow waves are also a hallmark of healthy sleep, where they are thought to serve restorative functions and to help maintain network homeostasis. Here, we asked whether slow waves in DoC can provide insights into the severity of cortical dysfunction or, conversely, into the preservation of residual awareness and recovery potential, by characterizing their spatial, temporal and morphological properties. We analyzed 256-channel EEG recorded during the ‘Local-Global’ auditory paradigm in 236 DoC patients, including 123 in Unresponsive Wakefulness Syndrome (UWS), 62 in a Minimally Conscious State minus (MCS-) and 29 in MCS plus (MCS+). High-amplitude slow waves were detected as local events, and their density, amplitude and slopes were quantified scalp-wide and channel-wise, by mean level and temporal variability across the recording. Slow-wave features were related to concurrent (Coma Recovery Scale-Revised - CRS-R, ‘Local-Global’ paradigm) and follow-up (one-year Glasgow Outcome Scale-Extended - GOS-E) assessments of consciousness and functional recovery. Associations with MRI-derived measures of cortical integrity and structural connectivity, and differences between anoxic and traumatic etiologies, were also examined. Slow waves were more frequent in less severe DoC patients (i.e., MCS) compared to UWS. Slow-wave slopes were also steeper frontally and increased at a faster rate in patients exhibiting higher levels of consciousness and/or markers of conscious access during the ‘Local-Global’ paradigm. More frequent and steeper slow waves at baseline further predicted recovery of consciousness and of functional independence at one year. At the individual level, slow-wave features classified states of consciousness above chance (AUC = 0.62 and 0.71 for MCS- and MCS+ versus UWS), whereas outcome prediction remained modest (0.56-0.58). These clinical associations had a structural basis: slow-wave occurrence was greater after traumatic than anoxic injury and scaled with the integrity of subcortico-cortical projection fibers and consciousness-related cortical networks. Contrary to the classical view of delta activity as a non-specific marker of impaired consciousness, prominent spontaneous slow waves indexed behavioral and neurophysiological signs of awareness and better recovery. Beyond reflecting a propensity for pathological bistability, they may also signal the integrity of the networks required to generate them, together with their residual capacity for dynamic regulation. <h4>Highlights</h4>  Spontaneous slow waves were detected as discrete local events in 236 DoC patients, moving beyond time-averaged spectral measures of cortical slowing. Slow-wave properties carried diagnostic information about the state of consciousness, whether defined behaviorally (CRS-R) or neurophysiologically (conscious access to auditory information in ‘Local-Global’ ERP paradigm). Slow wave frequency and slope during the acute phase in the ICU predicted improvement of consciousness state and recovery of functional independence at one year. Within-session ‘fatigue-like’ temporal modulation of slow waves increased with the level of consciousness recovery, being more pronounced in MCS+ than in UWS and MCS-patients. Slow-wave expression scaled with subcortico-cortical fibers and cortical network integrity, and was greater after traumatic than anoxic injury, suggesting they signal preserved network integrity rather than damage alone.

[PMID: 42775162] Calibration and transfer in indicator-based assessments of artificial consciousness.

Research on artificial consciousness increasingly shifts evaluation from behaviour to internal architecture. Theory-based indicators are used to update probability assignments. This improves on behavioural tests but raises two distinct problems. First, these assignments cannot currently be calibrated against independently established artificial consciousness outcomes. Second, their evidential relevance is transferred from biological cases without independent support that indicator-consciousness relations remain stable across substrates. This commentary distinguishes calibration from transfer and adapts the iterative natural-kind strategy by proposing a preliminary, theory-relative comparative space for cross-substrate assessment.

[PMID: 42908551] Development and validation of an interpretable machine learning model for predicting early acute kidney injury and altered consciousness in cardiac surgery patients admitted to the intensive care unit: a retrospective study.

<h4>Background</h4>Acute kidney injury (AKI) and altered consciousness are common and serious complications following cardiac surgery, both associated with increased morbidity and mortality in the intensive care unit (ICU). Early prediction of these complications is essential for timely intervention. We aimed to develop and validate an interpretable machine learning model for predicting early AKI and altered consciousness risk in this population.<h4>Methods</h4>We retrospectively extracted data from the MIMIC-IV database. Adult patients who underwent cardiac surgery within 24 h before ICU admission were included. The primary outcome was early AKI defined by KDIGO criteria within 48 h after ICU admission. The secondary outcome was altered consciousness within 24 h after ICU admission, defined as a Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) score ≤12. A comprehensive set of candidate variables was screened using four feature selection methods (LassoCV, mRMR, mutual information, and <i>F</i>-test) with a voting strategy. Eight machine learning models were developed and compared. SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) values were used to interpret the best-performing model. External validation was performed using data from cardiac surgery patients at Wuxi No.2 People's Hospital between 1 January 2020 and 31 December 2025.<h4>Result</h4>A total of 5,288 patients were included, with an AKI incidence of 76.2% (4,031/5,288) and an altered consciousness incidence of 22.8% (1,206/5,288). For the primary outcome of AKI, the XGBoost model achieved a training AUC of 0.787 (95% CI: 0.770-0.804) and an internal test AUC of 0.659 (95% CI: 0.629-0.688). For the secondary outcome of altered consciousness, the XGBoost model achieved an internal test AUC of 0.951 (95% CI: 0.936-0.963). Calibration curve and decision curve analysis confirmed good calibration and clinical utility for both models. SHAP analysis identified SpO<sub>2</sub>, age, APSIII score, creatinine, and white blood cell count as the top 5 most important predictors for AKI. External validation was performed in an independent cohort of 395 cardiac surgery ICU patients from Wuxi No.2 People's Hospital, in whom the incidence of AKI and altered consciousness was 28.1% and 67.3%, respectively. The models retained acceptable discrimination in this external cohort (AKI: AUC 0.764, 95% CI: 0.711-0.818; altered consciousness: AUC 0.679, 95% CI: 0.615-0.737).<h4>Conclusion</h4>We developed interpretable XGBoost-based prediction models for both early AKI and altered consciousness risk in cardiac surgery patients admitted to the ICU. The models exhibited excellent discrimination, calibration, and clinical utility, which may facilitate early identification of high-risk patients and guide personalized interventions.

[PMID: 41880257] Awareness- and Arousal-Specific Brain Analysis Predicts Spinal Cord Stimulation Effectiveness in Disorders of Consciousness.

Spinal cord stimulation (SCS) is an advanced treatment for disorders of consciousness (DoC), but its success rate varies between 30 and 60%, and consciousness-related biomarkers are urgently needed for SCS assessment and optimization. This paper proposes an awareness- and arousal-specific brain analysis (AAA) method to encode these two consciousness dimensions and construct consciousness-related features to predict SCS outcomes of DoC patients. Firstly, electroencephalogram (EEG) brain signals were collected from twenty-eight DoC patients during SCS treatment. Then, dynamic brain networks are formulated based on sliding-window correlation analysis of weighted phase lag index. Afterwards, a hierarchical network decomposition algorithm was developed to resolve the dynamic networks into consciousness-related networks by elaborating data-driven optimization of non-negative matrix factorization and consciousness-related variability. Further, consciousness features are designed to quantify the activation, interaction, and stability of awareness- and arousal-specific networks, and a support vector machine is trained for classification of SCS treatment effectiveness. Clinical results showed that our method achieved an overall prediction accuracy of 88%, which is significantly better than clinical accuracy of 50% by doctors. Moreover, our method outperformed existing EEG-based approaches in both prediction accuracy and pathology interpretability. The proposed awareness- and arousal-specific brain analysis method establishes a pivotal framework for precise and reliable SCS treatment of DoC.

[PMID: 42879878] Embodied consciousness: Reflections on anatomy, spirituality, and healing.

The human body is commonly approached through the language of anatomy, yet anatomy alone may not fully capture the lived experience of being embodied. This reflection explores the relationship between anatomical structure, embodied consciousness, spirituality, and healing through an osteopathic perspective. Drawing on osteopathic philosophy, phenomenology, fascial science, interoception, and embodied cognition, it considers the body not merely as an anatomical object but as a medium through which awareness and experience are expressed. [PMID: 42266156] The entropic brain today.

Introduced in 2014 and revised in 2018, the entropic brain hypothesis has accrued a wealth of supportive evidence. The hypothesis states that-along a dimension of 'the size of phenomenal consciousness'-expansive states reliably exhibit increased brain entropy, whereas the inverse applies for states of no or reduced consciousness. Examples of expansive states include expert meditation, flicker-light stimulation, near-death-like experiences, atypical breathing, REM sleep, the pre-ictal aura, unmedicated early psychosis and psychedelic drug states. Examples of states of no or reduced consciousness with low brain entropy, include disorders of consciousness, deep sleep, the anaesthetized state, seizure, post-stroke, ageing, cognitive impairment and neurodegenerative illness. It is shown that the entropic brain has convergent, correlative, predictive, discriminative and external validity. Regarding its predictive validity, increased brain entropy under psilocybin (in a supportive context) predicts subsequent improvements in mental health (improved well-being 1-month post-dose). Regarding its discriminative validity, changes in brain entropy selectively index the 'breadth of subjective experience' versus alternative dimensions, such as arousal. Regarding portability/external validity, an entropy-related function is applied in generative artificial intelligence. In conclusion, the entropic brain is a useful model of conscious states.

[PMID: 42876598] Can artificial systems become conscious?-Recursive embodied closure as an integrative framework.

Recent advances in artificial intelligence, connectomics, and computational neuroscience increasingly raise the question of whether artificial systems-or even pure computer simulations-could become conscious. Contemporary theories emphasize embodiment, predictive processing, homeostatic regulation, and continuous interaction between cognition, body, and environment rather than isolated computation. We propose recursive embodied closure as an integrative framework linking embodied, predictive, functionalist, and dynamical approaches to consciousness. Rather than replacing existing theories, the framework identifies a shared organizational principle: the continuous recursive coupling of cognition, bodily regulation, action, and environmental feedback. We argue that consciousness may, in principle, emerge in artificial systems, including computational simulations, for three reasons. First, consciousness may depend on recursively closed loops linking cognition, body, and environment, and such loops could also be realized in robotic, neuromorphic, or virtual embodiments. Second, evidence from comparative neuroscience, convergent evolution, neurotechnology, and philosophy of mind suggests that the mechanisms relevant for consciousness are largely substrate-independent and may therefore be implemented in both biological and artificial systems. Third, although unknown physical mechanisms cannot be excluded, there is currently no empirical evidence for biological processes that are uniquely necessary for consciousness. Taken together, these considerations support the view that consciousness is best understood as an emergent property of recursively embodied, environmentally embedded cognitive systems rather than of biological matter itself. If so, biological brains, artificial neural networks, neuromorphic hardware, and even virtual agents may constitute plausible candidates for future artificial consciousness.

Climate change, environmental degradation, and increasing pressure on health-care resources highlight the need for nurses who are prepared to promote planetary health and sustainable health care. This study examined nursing students' sustainability consciousness, including self-reported knowingness, attitudes, and stewardship-related behaviour, and their perceived planetary health competency, and explored associated sociodemographic and academic factors. An analytical cross-sectional study was conducted among 300 undergraduate nursing students at the College of Nursing, Prince Sattam bin Abdulaziz University, Saudi Arabia. As these outcomes were assessed using self-report measures, the findings reflect students' reported perceptions and behaviours rather than objectively observed knowledge or practices. Students demonstrated relatively high sustainability consciousness and moderate-to-high perceived planetary health competency. Overall sustainability consciousness was not significantly related to planetary health competency. Previous sustainability education was associated with higher sustainability consciousness, whereas the examined sociodemographic and academic characteristics were not significantly associated with planetary health competency. Nursing curricula should integrate sustainability and planetary health education across academic levels and provide experiential opportunities that connect environmental knowledge with sustainable health-care practices, stewardship, and climate-responsive nursing.

[PMID: 42780860] From detection to decision: a conceptual framework of uncertainty migration in disorders of consciousness.

Accurate assessment of consciousness in patients with disorders of consciousness (DoC) remains a longstanding challenge in neurocritical care and neurorehabilitation. Although standardized behavioral assessments continue to serve as the clinical foundation for diagnosis, their reliance on overt behavioral responses may underestimate residual consciousness in a subset of patients. Recent advances in multimodal neuroimaging and neurophysiological techniques have substantially expanded the capacity to detect covert consciousness, reducing some of the inherent limitations of behavioral assessment and facilitating the identification of patients with preserved cognitive processing despite an absence of observable responses. However, these technological advances have not eliminated uncertainty in DoC assessment. Instead, uncertainty may be reconfigured as assessment progresses from behavioral observation to neural measurement, the inference of conscious awareness, prognostic evaluation, and clinical decision-making. Whether neural activity can reliably reflect subjective conscious experience, how neurophysiological evidence should inform prognostic evaluation and therapeutic decisions, and the emerging risks introduced by artificial intelligence-assisted assessment all indicate that uncertainty in DoC has evolved beyond a purely technical challenge into a broader problem of interpretation and clinical decision-making. Artificial intelligence (AI) further intersects with these uncertainties across multiple stages by contributing to signal processing, multimodal integration, consciousness inference, prognostic prediction, and clinical decision support. In this review, we propose the conceptual framework of "uncertainty migration" to characterize how uncertainty may change in form, persist across epistemic transitions, be redistributed between stages or actors, and accumulate through interactions between residual and newly introduced uncertainties across five interconnected domains of DoC assessment: behavioral assessment, neural measurement, consciousness inference, prognostic evaluation and clinical decision-making. These processes of transformation, propagation, redistribution, and accumulation are neither mutually exclusive nor necessarily linear, as discordant multimodal findings and longitudinal changes may prompt the reassessment of earlier evidence. We argue that multimodal approaches fundamentally change how consciousness-related evidence is acquired, and may reduce particular sources of uncertainty without eliminating the inherently inferential nature of consciousness assessment. Consequently, future advances in the DoC field should extend beyond improving diagnostic accuracy toward developing comprehensive strategies for uncertainty management that emphasize rigorous interpretation of evidence, longitudinal reassessment, multimodal evidence integration, transparent AI governance,and shared decision-making. Such an approach may better support cautious, transparent, and patient-centered clinical decisions in the face of persistent uncertainty.

[PMID: ] Induction and Overreach in the Science of Consciousness

<p>Induction shapes consciousness science as researchers infer experience, generalizing neural signatures, and project across contexts. While deduction sets ideals of certainty, induction guides experimentation and decision-making. This article provides a synoptic analysis of induction’s role in consciousness science by surveying its philosophical background, from Aristotle and Hume to Popper, Salmon, and Peirce, and its formalization in Bayesian and predictive-processing frameworks. This manuscript then examines how induction operates across neuroimaging, comparative cognition, and clinical contexts, highlighting successes (pattern detection, anesthesia monitoring, covert awareness detection) and structural risks (correlation–causation slippage, overgeneralization, anthropomorphism, and high-stakes misclassification). Recent sufficiency-oriented syntheses (e.g., Coppola, 2025; Mashour et al., 2020; Hohwy et al., 2020) exemplify both the power and the perils of inductive generalization, extending correlational findings into claims of ontological sufficiency. Building on recent critiques and adversarial comparisons of leading theories, this article argues for an explicitly deflationary program that keeps induction indispensable but bounded. The core tools are: (i) operational vocabularies distinguishing constructs (access, arousal, valuation, metacognition); (ii) transparent bridge principles linking observables to experiences; and (iii) prediction tables that register both positive predictions and falsifiers. This pragmatic framework does not solve Hume’s problem; it makes our inductive commitments tractable, testable, and ethically responsible. Consciousness science should remain inductive, just not inflationary. To demonstrate this framework’s tractability, the paper introduces examples of bridge principles and quantitative criteria for “bounded” inference, including Bayesian priors, model-comparison metrics, and causal graphs. Ethical and institutional dimensions are expanded to include AI, animal, and algorithmic induction. These additions link philosophical modesty with methodological implementation and moral accountability.</p>

[PMID: ] Overcoming essentialism: evaluation of theories of consciousness by the lawful regularities they predict

<p>A common objection to consciousness research is that researchers cannot agree on what consciousness is. But how should a consensus on the nature of consciousness be reached? Does consciousness have essential features that one can be right or wrong about? Should we select theories of consciousness based on their descriptions of the essential features of experience alone? In this article, we will argue with the help of (Popper, 1945) that determining unquestionable essential features of consciousness is neither possible nor desirable. Instead, any account of what consciousness is, should be understood as a subjective selection of features and should not count as an insight. According to Popper, insights in science are achieved when novel regularities between observations are discovered that generalize to scientific laws. Furthermore, all results of research can be stated independently of the definitions that were used in their making. Therefore, no consensus on the nature of consciousness or on a theory of consciousness is needed for consciousness research to progress – in fact, a multitude of definitions and theories may be beneficial. Based on this, we argue that theories of consciousness be evaluated by their ability to propose concrete, testable laws rather than by their description of what consciousness is.</p>

[PMID: 42876783] Auditory paradigm-dependent EEG feature screening and representation for distinguishing consciousness states in male patients with disorders of consciousness.

<h4>Introduction</h4>Accurate assessment of disorders of consciousness (DoC), including minimally conscious state (MCS) and unresponsive wakefulness syndrome (UWS/VS), remains a major challenge in clinical neurorehabilitation. EEG-based biomarkers offer a non-invasive approach for characterizing residual brain activity, yet their discriminative sensitivity may depend on auditory stimulus design.<h4>Methods</h4>EEG recordings were obtained from 15 male patients with DoC during four auditory stimulation paradigms. Nonlinear, spectral power, and graph-theoretic features were extracted, yielding 136 candidate features. All individual features were independently evaluated using patient-level leave-one-out cross-validation (LOOCV) to characterize paradigm- and feature-domain-specific predictive performance. In a complementary exploratory optimization analysis, univariate statistical screening identified 26 features with nominal group differences (<i>p</i> < 0.05); these features were subsequently subjected to within-fold K-means clustering and principal component analysis (PCA), and compared with directly combined feature representations for MCS versus UWS/VS classification and CRS-R score regression.<h4>Results</h4>In the single-feature benchmark, the reversed-word sequence paradigm showed relatively higher averaged classification performance, while nonlinear features demonstrated comparatively stronger classification characteristics than spectral power and graph-theoretic features. In the optimization analysis, the PCA-derived representation achieved competitive classification performance, whereas the direct combination of all statistically screened features achieved the highest observed classification accuracy. Regression performance was generally limited.<h4>Discussion</h4>These findings suggest that auditory stimulus design and EEG feature representation may influence the discrimination of consciousness states in DoC. In this small-sample exploratory study, reversed-word auditory stimulation and nonlinear EEG features showed promising discriminative characteristics, while direct feature combination provided additional predictive information for categorical classification. Further validation in larger and independent cohorts is required.

[PMID: 42810504] Mnemonic emergence theory of consciousness: A hierarchical memory architecture in the organization for meaning, integration, and self.

Existing theories of consciousness insufficiently explain the neurocognitive mechanisms through which neural information acquires subjective meaning. To address this gap, we proposed the Mnemonic Emergence Theory (MET) of consciousness, which posits memory not as a passive repository but as an active, system-forming, and meaning/goal-generating basis of conscious behavior. In contrast to classical views, MET postulates that the dynamic organization of memory engrams, integrated with present motivational states, contextual frameworks, and predictive capabilities, constitutes the core mechanism by which meaning/goal emerges and consciousness is established. Within this framework, meaning is not an intrinsic property of information. It arises when current sensory input is integrated into a holistic relational system encompassing the subject's long-term experience, dominant motivation, and anticipated future outcomes. We proposed a functional system model wherein the memory apparatus serves as the integrative hub, linking all other key components of goal-directed behavior, while the ultimate system-forming factor remains the achievement of adaptive outcomes. MET suggests a hierarchical evolutionary model of memory - from simple synaptic traces and conditioned associations, through bidirectional connections and predictability, to self-reflective meta-memory, oscillatory integration, and finally, to an autobiographically extended conscious self. Consciousness, under this view, represents the function and operation of memory at its highest developmental level: a recursively structured, affectively laden, and temporally integrated composition. The central tenet of MET may be summarized as follows: from the memory engram to its actualization, through affective valence and predictive goals, towards temporal and oscillatory integration (meta-memory), and culminating in phenomenal consciousness.

[PMID: 42808207] The lost physiology of consciousness: Ernst Gellhorn and the origins of homeostatic neuroscience.

Contemporary neuroscience often presents modern theories of consciousness as products of the cognitive revolution and computational neuroscience. Yet several neurophysiologists before and during the early cybernetic period had already developed sophisticated physiological models of conscious states grounded in bodily regulation, autonomic integration, hypothalamic function and global neural activation. Among these figures, Ernest Gellhorn (1935-2005) occupies a remarkable place. In <i>Physiological Foundations of Neurology and Psychiatry</i> (1953), Gellhorn proposed an integrative neurophysiological theory linking consciousness, arousal, emotion, endocrine regulation, autonomic balance, sleep, and bodily homeostasis. This article argues that Gellhorn prefigured several dimensions of contemporary consciousness research, especially embodied, affective, and homeostatic approaches. It also situates him within the broader neurophysiological framework represented at the 1953 Laurentian Symposium, where Giuseppe Moruzzi (1910-1986), Horace Winchell Magoun (1907-1991), and Denise-Albe Fessard (1916-2003) emphasized reticular activation and large-scale nervous integration. The comparison has clear limits: Gellhorn did not anticipate the anatomically defined functional networks revealed by modern neuroimaging, the information-broadcast architecture of global neuronal workspace theory, or the philosophical "hard problem" of why physiological processes are accompanied by subjective experience. Rather than claiming direct historical continuity, the study identifies structural conceptual convergences between mid-twentieth-century neurophysiology and selected current theories of consciousness.

[PMID: 42870387] Residual Language Abilities Correlate With Diagnosis and Prognosis in Patients With Disorders of Consciousness.

Additionally, 38.10% (8 out of 21) of UWS patients and 33.33% (8 out of 24) of MCS patients demonstrated significant ITPC at the phrase rate. The language paradigm performance in this study was superior to that of other paradigms in detecting the proportion of residual language ability in DoC patients. Furthermore, cortical tracking at the phrase rate significantly correlated with 12-month outcomes and effectively classified patients according to their 12-month prognosis.<h4>Conclusions</h4>The simplified language assessment demonstrates strong potential to detect residual language ability in DoC patients and could potentially be used to detect residual consciousness and determine prognosis in unresponsive patients.

[PMID: 42865305] Effects of General Anaesthesia on the Sleep Structure of Patients With Prolonged Disorders of Consciousness.

Sleep disturbances are common in patients with prolonged disorders of consciousness (pDoC) and are closely correlated with clinical outcomes. With an increasing number of pDoC patients undergoing surgery under general anaesthesia (GA), this study characterized sleep patterns in pDoC patients and evaluated the effects of GA on consciousness and nocturnal sleep. We prospectively enrolled 30 pDoC patients (14 in vegetative state [VS] and 16 in minimally conscious state [MCS]) undergoing spinal cord stimulation and 12 healthy controls. Among pDoC patients, 19 (10 VS, 9 MCS) with complete pre- and postoperative PSG data were analysed for GA effects. Controls underwent one overnight polysomnography (PSG); pDoC patients underwent pre- and postoperative PSG. Consciousness was assessed with Coma Recovery Scale-Revised (CRS-R) before and after surgery. Compared with controls, pDoC patients presented significantly impaired sleep structure, including reduced total sleep time (TST), decreased NREM Stage 2 sleep, diminished slow-wave sleep (SWS), reduced rapid eye movement (REM) sleep duration and percentage relative to TST (all p < 0.05). No significant short-term changes in CRS-R scores were observed following GA. Although GA significantly prolonged TST (p = 0.039), with numerical increases in absolute NREM and SWS durations, no statistically significant changes were observed in sleep stage percentages relative to TST (all p > 0.05). Subgroup analysis showed no significant within-group changes in sleep stage percentages in either the VS or MCS group. These findings suggest that GA increases TST without statistically significant changes in sleep structure in pDoC patients.

[PMID: 42822076] AI is not as conscious as humans: Essentialism in mental state attributions to artificial systems.

Artificial systems increasingly behave in ways that resemble humans. But is that sufficient to make us see them as conscious, or as having related mental states? We contrast two broad possibilities. People may rely on a single general principle: the more appropriately an agent responds to its environment, the more conscious it is, whether human or artificial. We call this a functionalist attribution. Alternatively, people may reserve consciousness for certain kinds of beings: even an artificial system that behaves just as responsively as a human is judged less conscious. This is an essentialist attribution. A further question is whether this pattern is unique to consciousness or extends to closely related constructs such as awareness. In two pre-registered sets of studies (five experiments, N = 1092), UK participants read scenarios describing human or artificial agents whose responsiveness to spatial, social, and temporal features of the environment varied, and rated the agents' consciousness and awareness, either separately or in conjunction. Attributions of consciousness followed an essentialist logic: at similar levels of responsiveness, participants consistently attributed greater consciousness to humans. Attributions of awareness followed a functionalist pattern: for both humans and artificial agents, awareness increased steadily with responsiveness, although humans were rated more aware at the highest levels. These findings suggest that people use different inferential rules for different mental concepts. Consciousness is treated as depending on what an agent is; awareness largely on what an agent does.

[PMID: 42756023] AI models as consciousness attributors: how LLMs ascribe consciousness to other agents.

Research on AI consciousness has largely focused on whether AI systems are conscious and how humans attribute consciousness to them. Yet large language models (LLMs) increasingly function as consciousness attributors, generating judgments about whether and to what degree other entities are conscious. We introduce model-generated consciousness attribution as an object of empirical operationalization and diagnosis, defining an attribution rule as the recurring relationship between features of a target and an evaluator's ratings, without implying subjective belief, intention, or experience. An illustrative probe compared the attribution patterns of nine contemporary models with a human reference. Nearly all model runs occupied the same region of the human-derived measurement space, characterized by comparatively strong, positive weighting of metacognitive self-reflection. The models also produced broadly similar rankings of fictional AI characters from movies, while differing in their overall rating levels. We propose a diagnostic agenda organized around three questions: how model attribution is oriented relative to human references, how attribution rules vary across models, and how observed patterns depend on the cue sets, targets, and task formats through which they are measured. As LLM-generated judgments circulate through public, professional, and academic settings, diagnosing these attribution rules can help characterize how AI systems participate in shaping interpretations of AI consciousness. This remains distinct from the ontological question of whether the systems themselves are conscious.

[PMID: ] Pluralism within limits: How to make the idea of multiple NCC kinds useful

<p>Recently, several authors have argued that the current impasse in consciousness science supports a pluralistic interpretation: the view that seemingly contradictory findings may both be valid because consciousness can be brought about in different ways, each sufficient to generate conscious experience. We analyze different versions of pluralism to determine under what conditions it offers a viable interpretation of consciousness science. We argue that viable pluralism requires a two-factor framework distinguishing general consciousness factors (mechanisms rendering contents conscious) from content encoding itself. Moreover, NCC kinds must be finite in number and systematically ordered to enable a unified scientific model. While pluralism enables testable hypotheses, significant ontological tensions arise: how can consciousness constitute a unified natural kind if realized by fundamentally distinct NCC kinds? Drawing on philosophy of science, we propose an alternative interpretation in terms of scientific rather than natural kinds. Our goal is not to advocate for pluralism, but rather, to critically examine its viability and implications. Based on this, we urge the field to engage more explicitly with the foundational assumptions that shape consciousness research.</p>

[PMID: 42890011] The mediating role of organizational justice in the relationship between cost consciousness and resource efficiency among human service professionals.

<h4>Background</h4>Organizational psychology research suggests that employees' perceptions of workplace practices play an important role in shaping organizational outcomes. In resource-constrained Human Service Organizations (HSOs), organizational justice may represent a key psychological mechanism linking cost-conscious practices to resource-efficient behaviors.<h4>Objective</h4>This study examined whether organizational justice explains the association between cost consciousness and resource efficiency among professionals working in Human Service Organizations (HSOs).<h4>Methods</h4>A cross-sectional survey design was employed with 246 professionals working in public and municipal human service institutions in Türkiye, including social workers, nurses, and administrative personnel. Data were analyzed using confirmatory factor analysis and regression-based mediation analysis with Hayes' PROCESS Macro (Model 4) and 5,000 bootstrap resamples.<h4>Results</h4>Cost consciousness was positively associated with both organizational justice perception (<i>β</i> = 0.500, <i>p</i> < 0.001) and resource efficiency (<i>β</i> = 0.466, <i>p</i> < 0.001). Organizational justice was also positively associated with resource efficiency (<i>β</i> = 0.207, <i>p</i> = 0.001). Mediation analysis indicated that organizational justice partially explained the association between cost consciousness and resource efficiency (indirect effect = 0.104, 95% CI [0.047-0.160]). The final model explained 37.7% of the variance in resource efficiency.<h4>Conclusion</h4>The findings suggest that cost-conscious practices in human service settings may contribute more effectively to sustainable resource use when employees perceive organizational processes as fair and transparent. Strengthening organizational justice may therefore support workforce-related efficiency and sustainable service delivery in public and social care systems.

[PMID: ] TMS-EEG Investigation of Cortical Activity Induced by Individualized rTMS in Patients with Prolonged Disorders of Consciousness

<title>Abstract</title>  <p>  Objective  To establish an individualized repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation (rTMS) target selection protocol based on resting-state electroencephalography (EEG) directed functional connectivity for patients with prolonged disorders of consciousness (pDoC), and to explore the underlying electrophysiological mechanisms using transcranial magnetic stimulation combined with EEG (TMS-EEG). Methods  We enrolled 42 pDoC patients and randomly assigned to an individualized rTMS group, a conventional rTMS group, and a sham stimulation group. The individualized rTMS group selected the target with stronger directed functional connectivity between F3 and Pz as the stimulation site based on Granger causality analysis. The conventional rTMS group received random stimulation at either F3 or Pz. Both active groups received 10 sessions of high-frequency rTMS over 10 consecutive days, while the sham group underwent sham stimulation. Behavioral improvement and cortical connectivity were assessed before and after treatment using the Coma Recovery Scale-Revised (CRS-R) and TMS-EEG, respectively. Results  CRS-R scores significantly increased in both the individualized and conventional rTMS groups (  <italic>p</italic>   < 0.05), whereas no significant improvement was observed in the sham group (  <italic>p</italic>   > 0.05). TMS-EEG analysis demonstrated that the amplitudes of N100 and P180 components significantly increased post-treatment in both active groups (  <italic>p</italic>   < 0.05), with no statistically significant changes in the sham group (  <italic>p</italic>   > 0.05). Global mean field amplitude (GMFA) significantly increased post-treatment in both active groups (  <italic>p</italic>   < 0.05). Specifically, significant increases were found in the 1-100 ms and 101–200 ms time windows post-treatment in both active groups (  <italic>p</italic>   < 0.05). Additionally, the individualized rTMS group showed a significant increase in the 201–300 ms window (  <italic>p</italic>   < 0.05), which was not observed in the conventional group (  <italic>p</italic>   > 0.05). No significant differences were found in the sham group across any time windows (1-100 ms, 101–200 ms, 201–300 ms, 301–400 ms, 401–500 ms;  <italic>p</italic>   > 0.05). Correlation analyses indicated that the change in CRS-R score positively correlated with the change in N100 amplitude in both the individualized (  <italic>r</italic>   = 0.82,  <italic>p</italic>   < 0.001) and conventional (  <italic>r</italic>   = 0.79,  <italic>p</italic>   = 0.001) treatment groups. Similarly, the change in CRS-R score positively correlated with the change in P180 amplitude in the individualized (  <italic>r</italic>   = 0.77,  <italic>p</italic>   = 0.0002) and conventional (  <italic>r</italic>   = 0.62,  <italic>p</italic>   = 0.025) groups. Conclusion  Individualized rTMS based on resting-state EEG directed functional connectivity is more effective in promoting consciousness recovery in pDoC patients. Compared to conventional rTMS, this approach may better facilitate the sustained propagation of post-stimulation cortical activity along residual consciousness networks and promote integration during middle-to-late stages. This study provides preliminary evidence supporting the transition of neuromodulation therapy for pDoC from fixed-target interventions to individualized precision regulation. Trial registration:  ClinicalTrials.gov, NCT05682248.  <bold>Ethics approval</bold>  : Ethics Committee of Xuanwu Hospital, 2022[022].  </p>

[PMID: 42853683] Understanding consciousness (biological and artificial) as a society of self-schemas.

This article argues that human cognition is more than the performance of functions such as memory, decision-making, and attention. The brain also builds schematic models of those cognitive functions and uses those models to monitor, predict, and partially control them. When you introspect, your understanding of who you are and what is happening inside of you is derived from those schematic, sometimes inaccurate, but generally useful models, rather than from the actual cognitive functions that are represented by those models. The proposal expands the previously proposed attention schema theory to a more general schema theory, in which the mind as we know it introspectively is a society of interacting self-schemas. The article focuses on five self-schemas including the attention schema (which is proposed to give us our intuitions about a conscious mind that takes possession of items in a vivid manner), a decision schema (which generates simplified, sometimes confabulated accounts of the decision-making process and gives us our intuitive understanding of agency and will), emotion schemas (which create complex interpretations of the physiological state of the body and provide us with our understanding of emotional experience), the body schema (which provides our understanding of who we are as physical beings), and personas (which give us our understanding of our emotional, social, and moral character, and act as a framework to guide our behavior). The article includes a discussion of how consciousness in a machine requires a similar suite of self-schemas used for similar functional purposes and may already be partially present.

[PMID: 42755652] An environmental psychology model of sustainability in sport: the roles of sustainability consciousness, environmental awareness, and personal social responsibility.

<h4>Background</h4>Understanding the psychological processes associated with sustainable behavior has become a major focus of environmental psychology. Although sustainability consciousness is generally associated with environmentally responsible behavior, the psychological processes underlying this association in sport remain insufficiently understood, particularly among sport science students. Therefore, the present study examined the serial mediating roles of environmental awareness and personal social responsibility in the association between sustainability consciousness and sustainability in sport.<h4>Methods</h4>A cross-sectional correlational design was employed. The study included 521 undergraduate students enrolled in Faculties of Sport Sciences at six universities in Türkiye during the 2025-2026 academic year. Participants completed the sustainability consciousness scale, environmental awareness scale, personal social responsibility scale, and sustainability in sport scale. Serial mediation analysis was performed using Hayes' PROCESS Macro (Model 6) with 5,000 bootstrap resamples. Prior to hypothesis testing, the assumptions required for regression analyses, including normality, multicollinearity, and the absence of influential cases, were examined and found to be satisfactory.<h4>Results</h4>Sustainability consciousness was positively associated with environmental awareness, personal social responsibility, and sustainability in sport. Multiple regression analyses indicated that sustainability consciousness was positively associated with sustainability in sport. The serial mediation analysis further showed that sustainability consciousness was positively associated with environmental awareness and personal social responsibility, environmental awareness was positively associated with personal social responsibility, and both environmental awareness and personal social responsibility were positively associated with sustainability in sport. The direct association between sustainability consciousness and sustainability in sport remained statistically significant after accounting for the proposed mediators, suggesting partial mediation. Bootstrap analyses further indicated statistically significant indirect associations involving environmental awareness, personal social responsibility, and the sequential pathway including both constructs, as none of the 95% bootstrap confidence intervals included zero. These findings support the proposed serial mediation model as a theoretically informed pattern of statistical associations rather than evidence of causal mediation.<h4>Conclusion</h4>The findings suggest that sustainability consciousness, environmental awareness, personal social responsibility, and sustainability in sport are statistically associated in a manner consistent with the proposed environmental psychology framework. By examining these constructs within a serial mediation model, the present study contributes to the environmental psychology literature by providing a theoretically grounded perspective on the statistical associations underlying sustainability-related orientations within the sport context. The findings also have practical implications for the development of sustainability education and environmental responsibility initiatives within Faculties of Sport Sciences and provide a foundation for future longitudinal and experimental research examining these associations.

[PMID: 42793404] Awakening the Vagus: Transcutaneous Auricular Vagus Nerve Stimulation-A Narrative Review.

Disorders of consciousness (DoC), including unresponsive wakefulness syndrome/vegetative state (UWS/VS) and minimally conscious state (MCS), remain a major challenge in clinical neurology. Therapeutic options are limited, and prognosis is often uncertain. Transcutaneous stimulation of the auricular branch of the vagus nerve (taVNS) is a non-invasive neuromodulation technique that has gained increasing interest as a potential strategy to promote recovery of consciousness. This narrative review summarizes the anatomical and functional rationale of taVNS, compares invasive and non-invasive vagus nerve stimulation techniques, reviews the biological mechanisms underlying its effects, discusses established clinical applications, technological innovations, and available clinical trial evidence. Current data suggest that taVNS is safe and well-tolerated, with preliminary evidence of applicability in DoC, particularly in patients with MCS. However, heterogeneity in stimulation protocols and the limited number of large-scale randomized controlled trials highlight the need for further standardized research.

[PMID: 42691051] The mechanism of fashion consciousness influencing Gen Z's Willingness to continue using slow fashion apparel in a sustainable context.

To explore the mechanisms underlying Gen Z consumers' willingness to continue using slow fashion apparel, this study constructs a conceptual model based on SOR theory. The model considers personal and social fashion consciousness as stimulus variables, aesthetic, emotional, social, and green values as organism variables, and willingness to continue using as the response variable. Data were collected through a questionnaire based on a 7-point Likert scale, yielding 311 valid responses. SPSS and JASP were used to assess reliability and validity and examine direct and mediation effects. The results indicate that personal fashion consciousness, social value, and green value are all significantly and positively associated with willingness to continue using slow fashion apparel. Personal fashion consciousness is positively associated with perceived aesthetic and emotional values, whereas social fashion consciousness is positively associated with perceived social and green values. Furthermore, social and green values mediate the relationship between social fashion consciousness and willingness to continue using slow fashion apparel. However, the mediating effects of aesthetic and emotional values are not significant. This study suggests that in the context of sustainable consumption, value-related factors may better explain long-term use behavior than aesthetic experiences alone. These findings suggest that slow fashion brands should support individual expression and sustainability-related meanings and enhance Gen Z consumers' perceptions of green and social value, thereby encouraging continued use of slow fashion apparel and promoting sustainable fashion development.

[PMID: 42796315] Differences in the Subcortical White Matter Integrity Based on the Disorder of Consciousness at the Chronic Stage of Diffuse Axonal Injury: A Tract-Based Spatial Statistics Study.

<i>Background and Objectives</i>: In this study, we investigate the relationship between the state of consciousness and the whole white matter integrity at the chronic stage in diffuse axonal injury (DAI) patients, using tract-based spatial statistics (TBSS). <i>Materials and Methods</i>: Twenty-seven chronic DAI patients and 22 healthy controls were enrolled. Patients were divided into a disorder of consciousness (DOC) group (<i>n</i> = 11, Coma Recovery Scale-Revised [CRS-R] score < 23) and a non-DOC group (<i>n</i> = 16, CRS-R score = 23). Fractional anisotropy (FA) values across 48 subcortical white matter regions of interest (ROIs), defined by the Johns Hopkins University atlas, were analyzed using TBSS to evaluate group differences. <i>Results</i>: The groups significantly differed on FA values of 48 ROIs (Pillai's trace = 1.000, <i>p</i> < 0.05). The Tukey's HSD test revealed that the FA values of 44 ROIs (except for the column and body of fornix, right corticospinal tract, left retrolenticular part of internal capsule, and left uncinate fasciculus) among the 48 ROIs of the DOC group were significantly lower than those of the non-DOC group, whereas the FA values of all 48ROIs of the DOC group were significantly lower than those of the control group (<i>p</i> < 0.05). On the other hand, except for the bilateral corticospinal tract, the FA values of 46 ROIs among the 48 ROIs of the non-DOC group were significantly lower than those of the control group (<i>p</i> < 0.05). <i>Conclusions</i>: In DAI patients, the state of consciousness was related to injury severity in most subcortical neural structures.

[PMID: 42751830] Hitting and missing on consciousness.

Disturbances of conscious experience are central features of many psychiatric and mental health disorders. Experimental studies of conscious perception are therefore increasingly cited in clinical and translational work under the assumption that they identify neural mechanisms specific to subjective experience. Over the past decades, recording and analysis techniques have advanced substantially and many candidate neural markers of consciousness have been proposed; however, these markers may not genuinely reflect consciousness per se unless the underlying experimental paradigms are sufficiently stringent. We focus on one of the most widely used paradigms in basic and clinical research on consciousness: contrasting successful and unsuccessful detection of near-threshold sensory stimuli ("Hit & Miss" paradigms). Reporting stimulus presence depends not only on subjective experience but also on decision criteria that can vary independently of any genuine conscious threshold, as well as on fluctuations in perceptual processing capacity and prestimulus cognitive states. We explain why proposed refinements, such as the Perceptual Awareness Scale, binocular rivalry, and no-report paradigms, do not fully resolve these problems, and why many putative "neural correlates of consciousness" may instead index more general cognitive/perceptual capacities. These confounds have important implications for how basic consciousness research is interpreted in relation to hallucinations, flashbacks, and other disorders of experience. We outline alternative experimental strategies, inspired in part by blindsight and related dissociation paradigms, that more cleanly separate conscious perception from decision criteria and perceptual capacity, and may therefore provide a more reliable foundation for linking neural data to subjective experience in psychiatry and clinical neuroscience.

[PMID: 42785472] From neural propagation to conscious experience: A geometro-diffusion theory of consciousness.

Theories of consciousness frequently emphasize integration, differentiation, recurrent processing, or global availability, but the mathematical organization connecting neural propagation with subjective experience remains unclear. We introduce the Geometro-Diffusion Theory (GDT), in which diffusion-like dynamics on a structured substrate induces an intrinsic relational geometry. We introduce the Diffusion-Based Machine (DBM) as an operational model designed to distinguish information obtained from a single realized activity pattern from invariant information encoded across the family of possible dynamical responses. We show that independent diffusion realizations generate a Euclidean embedding whose Gram matrix is the diffusion kernel. This construction reveals metric relations, equivalence classes, symmetries, and globally organized routes that may remain inaccessible from an individual realization. To quantify this distinction, we define a partition gap comparing observational and geometric classifications and a normalized signature difference measuring their respective temporal differentiation. The central hypothesis is that conscious experience requires an intrinsic mechanism capable of accessing, integrating, and recursively using invariants of a sufficiently rich diffusion-induced geometry. Diffusion or geometry alone is therefore insufficient. We discuss how recurrent neural interactions, continuous activity, population integration, feedback, and plasticity could implement the required geometric access without explicit mathematical computation. The framework is summarized by six principles specifying the substrate, propagation dynamics, emergence and timescale of geometry, intrinsic decoding, and recursive integration. GDT provides operational quantities that can be examined using perturbational neural data across wakefulness, sleep, anesthesia, and disorders of consciousness.

[PMID: 42739231] Beside Resting-State EEG in the Diagnosis and Prognosis of Patients with Disorders of Consciousness.

<b>Background/Objectives</b>: Behavioral scales are the primary diagnostic tool for disorders of consciousness (DOC) but carry a high risk of misdiagnosis. Bedside resting-state EEG (rs-EEG) enables non-invasive objective brain function evaluation, although its diagnostic and prognostic value for DOC remains understudied. <b>Methods</b>: This study enrolled 207 DOC patients with guardian-signed informed consent. Weekly bedside 4-channel rs-EEG and CRS-R behavioral assessments were conducted during hospitalization. We systematically compared three categories of EEG metrics: nonlinear entropies (PE, SamEn), phase coupling parameters, and functional connectivity indices (SI, wPLI), evaluating their ability to stratify consciousness and forecast outcomes. <b>Results</b>: Permutation entropy (PE) stood out with triple functions: cross-sectional consciousness grading, longitudinal dynamic tracking, and prognosis prediction, surpassing all other EEG features. SamEn merely discriminates VS/UWS from MCS/EMCS but fails to separate MCS and EMCS. Power spectra, phase coupling, SI, and wPLI only yield group discrepancies at limited leads or frequency bands with unstable stratification, acting merely as auxiliary markers. Subtype-specific prognostic signatures were identified: frontal β-wPLI and parietal PE predict consciousness recovery in VS/UWS, whereas fronto-parietal α-wPLI predicts improvement in MCS patients. <b>Conclusions</b>: The bedside rs-EEG reliably assesses consciousness and predicts DOC outcomes, and PE possesses prominent clinical value for DOC diagnosis and prognostic stratification.

[PMID: ] What is there to explain about free will? An “easy problem” of consciousness downstream of agency

<p>Free will is one of the most enduring problems in philosophy and cognitive science, yet what, exactly, requires explanation remains unclear. The most tractable target is the experience of free will – the vivid sense of self-authorship, control, and a felt capacity to have done otherwise. As with the scientific study of mystical experiences, this phenomenology can be investigated without resolving the metaphysical claims implied by its content. Here, I argue that free will is an “easy problem” of consciousness: a phenomenon that can be measured, manipulated, predicted, and mechanistically explained. Taking causal determinism as a working postulate, I consider whether prediction and reinforcement could generate the experience of controlling one’s own actions through causal misattribution and superstitious conditioning, without granting consciousness independent causal power. If the experience can be explained under deterministic constraints, the core scientific explanandum of the free will phenomenon has been addressed. Free will can then be distinguished from agency, which remains a causally consequential, graded capacity for goal-directed, adaptive, and self-regulating action across living systems, machines, and hybrids thereof.</p>

[PMID: 42753650] Quantification of brain state into configuration entropy and complexity.

An objective measure of conscious and unconscious brain states is highly needed in the diagnosis of disorders of consciousness and in advancing the science of consciousness. However, its measurement based on the physical mechanism underlying its occurrence is still challenging. Our study aimed to quantify the electroencephalography (EEG) recording data under conscious state (awake, baseline and no seizure) and reduced consciousness or awareness (sleep, moderate sedation, seizure) into configuration entropy, entropy-complexity, and Gibbs-inspired free-energy. The study utilized the EEG datasets from public open-source repositories for analysis. The datasets were analyzed by using configuration entropy, permutation entropy, Lempel-Ziv complexity and Gibbs' formulae. Our findings revealed that the full waking (awake, baseline, no seizure) states exhibit significantly higher configuration entropy and entropy-complexity than states associated with reduced consciousness or awareness (sleep, sedation, and seizure). This suggests that the conscious brain state contains rich information capacity and is sustained by collective activity of neurons. We also find that the conscious state is associated with a low Gibbs-inspired free-energy index compared to the reduced state of consciousness or awareness. The reduced free-energy index enhances the formation of ordered pattern functional networks, which are essential in maintaining conscious awareness.

[PMID: ] Testable Specifications of Single-Synaptic Conscious Communication Through Phasic Signalling; Allowing Conscious Ontogenetic Development Through Synaptic Density, and Conscious Processing Within Subconscious Priors of Tonic Stimulation

The communication of consciousness between neurons within a single synapse is an underspecified occurrence, chemical transmission is essential for neural propagation, and neural propagation is essential within many existing measurements of consciousness. The development and plasticity of consciousness throughout an animal's lifetime requires grounded chemical distinctions of executive and non-executive processing and transmission. This paper's hypotheses propose chemical-conscious correlates between neurons, considered alongside established cognitive and environmental interaction, to specify how consciousness and its continuum are chemically produced at global scales, and developed ontogenetically during continuous environmental rationalization and neural propagation. Single-synaptic phasic transmitter transients may have evolved to allow executive function and selective consolidation within reflexive processing. These transmitter releases may be the chemical events through which subconscious stimulation allows phenomenal expression between neurons. This framing is consistent with phasic dynamics and tonic modulation of baseline synaptic stimulation, with each transmitter being differentiated by function. Phasic transients are further suggested to direct cortical spreading under tonic, epigenetic, and synaptic allowance. Because phasic releases fundamentally affect synaptic plasticity, while synapses determine the threshold and magnitude of phasic responses, this framing allows a developmental and individually variable model of consciousness. This development has potential to ground GWT/GNWT, IIT, Predictive Processing, and Affective Neuroscience, extending each framework by approaching phasic response as a mechanism of conscious variation and transmission at the scale of individual synapses. Because each hypothesis is specific and falsifiable, provided predictions and experimental directions can assist to determine the accuracy of each claim; given hypotheses are precise enough to fail and many of their correlating experiments are practical enough to proceed. If this account holds, it points toward practical improvements in depression treatments, therapeutics, memory rehabilitation, learning environment refinement strategies, and early developmental interventions.

[PMID: 42750545] The error theory of LLM consciousness: there is no evidence that standard LLMs are conscious.

Are chatbots AI zombies or conscious minds? I suspect they are AI zombies, at least when implemented on today's standard hardware. I argue that today's Large Language Mode's (LLM's) consciousness-like behaviors do not suggest they are conscious, because there is an error theory - a theory explaining why they behave as if they are conscious in absence of actual felt experience. LLMs function as "crowdsourced neocortices" - as they scale up, they come to mirror human conceptual structures, leading to human-like behaviors, including those involving consciousness. By contrast, bio-computers, quantum computers, and neuromorphic systems present more serious candidates for AI consciousness.

[PMID: 42839998] Who am I? Self-inquiry as a neurophenomenological approach to consciousness.

Inconclusive evidence for leading third-person theories of consciousness has renewed scientific interest in its first-person nature. Self-inquiry, a first-person method from Advaita Vedanta, can be operationalized to meet this need, though it has not received prior empirical study, likely owing to Advaita's idealism. By bracketing Advaita's metaphysical commitments and mapping its characterization of consciousness onto the distinctions drawn by consciousness science, self-inquiry can be understood as a systematic phenomenology of selfhood. The method's rationale is that mental contents perpetuate the "narrative self," or one's personal identity, so tracing such contents back to the sense of "I" to which they are referred attenuates the narrative self and discloses the "minimal self." The minimal self is the pre-reflective first-person perspective of experience that carries a sense of "for-me-ness." Sustained attention to the minimal self may then facilitate a selfless mode of awareness, or nondual awareness, marked by the absence of felt division between self and world. On this account, the narrative self and the appropriative ownership underlying the minimal self are seen as illusory, while an impersonal, reflexive consciousness remains. This deconstruction of selfhood already has neurophenomenological precedent, though from studies confined to Buddhist practice and reliant on idiosyncratic techniques. Self-inquiry thus holds empirical significance: its stepwise structure offers potential reproducibility across participants, a within-practice control, and a target attentional operation whose proposed distinctiveness can be empirically tested. It may also hold clinical relevance, having been proposed for incorporation into psychotherapy amid growing research into the therapeutic potential of self-attenuation.

[PMID: 42870908] Effects of single-modality sensory stimulation on brain function in patients with prolonged disorders of consciousness: a near-infrared spectroscopy-based study.

<h4>Objective</h4>To explore the effects of four single-modality sensory stimulations on the brain functional networks in patients with prolonged disorders of consciousness (pDOC), and to clarify the correlation between different sensory stimulations and the improvement of patients' consciousness level, so as to provide references for clinical treatment and rehabilitation of pDOC patients.<h4>Methods</h4>A prospective, multicenter, randomized controlled trial design was adopted. A total of 57 patients with pDOC were enrolled and divided into visual stimulation group, auditory stimulation group, olfactory-gustatory stimulation group, somatosensory-nocieptive stimulation group, and control group. The intervention duration of each group was 15 min. Behavioral assessments were performed using the Coma Recovery Scale-Revised (CRS-R) and Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS). Combined with functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS), hemodynamic parameters and brain functional connectivity were detected. Changes in brain function and consciousness level before and after different sensory stimulation interventions were evaluated.<h4>Results</h4>Visual, auditory and olfactory-gustatory stimulations induced significant and robust alterations in cerebral activity across most detected channels (<i>p</i> < 0.001), while the cerebral response to somatosensory-nocieptive stimulation was relatively limited (<i>p</i> < 0.05). At the channel activation level, viewing family photographs in visual stimulation, light music, personalized music and family voice calling in auditory stimulation, and acetic acid stimulation in olfactory-gustatory stimulation significantly activated the corresponding brain regions (<i>p</i> < 0.05). At the brain functional connectivity level, viewing family photographs markedly enhanced functional connectivity within visual-related brain regions (<i>p</i> < 0.05); ethanol stimulation improved functional connectivity of olfactory-gustatory brain regions (<i>p</i> < 0.05).<h4>Conclusion</h4>Single-modality sensory stimulation elicits acute cerebral activation in pDOC patients, which is superior to conventional rehabilitation alone. Visual and auditory stimulations exert significantly better intervention effects than olfactory-gustatory and somatosensory-nocieptive stimulations. Among olfactory-gustatory interventions, acetic acid stimulation produces prominent brain region activation, and ethanol enhances functional connectivity of related brain regions. Both can serve as an effective supplementary intervention for pDOC patients with visual and auditory dysfunction, and possess important value for further research and clinical application in the management of pDOC.
