[PMID: ] Psychedelics Restructure the Global Workspace of Consciousness

<h4>ABSTRACT</h4>  Psychoactive drugs profoundly alter consciousness, but the neural mechanisms by which they do so remain poorly understood. Global workspace theory may provide a framework for investigating these mechanisms. The global workspace, a candidate neural substrate of consciousness, is a set of brain regions that integrate and disseminate information to the rest of the brain. According to a recent model, the integrators, or “gateways,” are characterized by synergistic interactions with other brain regions, while the disseminators, or “broadcasters,” exhibit redundant interactions. Here, we measured the effects of various psychoactive drugs, including “classic” psychedelics like LSD, mescaline, and psilocybin, “atypical” psychedelics (entactogens) like MDMA, and the stimulant D-amphetamine, on the global workspace. In a secondary analysis of resting-state functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) data on all these drugs, we found that classic psychedelics are associated with significant increases in the number of gateways in the frontoparietal control and dorsal attention networks. Meanwhile, MDMA and amphetamine reduced the number of gateways in the visual and somatomotor networks. The regional topographies of both synergy and redundancy distinguished classic psychedelics from other drugs. Finally, drugs producing similar changes in synergy and redundancy also produced similar profiles of subjective effects. Overall, our findings provide the first direct evidence that psychedelics may alter the synergistic and redundant interactions that characterize the global workspace of consciousness.

[PMID: 42667420] Spatial spectral narrowing in the corticothalamic neural field model across consciousness states.

The neural correlates of consciousness have been characterized primarily through temporal EEG features, while the spatial dynamics of cortical activity across consciousness states remain poorly understood. Here, we analyze the spatial mode structure of the Robinson corticothalamic neural field model (CTM) across five consciousness states: healthy wakefulness, emergence from minimally conscious state (eMCS), minimally conscious state (MCS), deep sleep (N3), and unresponsive wakefulness syndrome (UWS). We compute the noise-amplified spatial spectrum, which quantifies how the CTM filters spatiotemporal noise across spatial wavenumbers. We find systematic spectral narrowing with decreasing consciousness: the spectral centroid decreases by 48% from healthy wakefulness to UWS, and the finite-wavenumber power fraction tracks the consciousness axis closely. A complementary dispersion analysis confirms that no classical Turing instability occurs in the CTM for any consciousness state. Two-dimensional stochastic simulations show that healthy cortex develops rich multi-scale spatial heterogeneity, while UWS exhibits markedly reduced spatial variation. These results provide a new spatial perspective on the CTM's representation of consciousness states and generate testable predictions for high-density EEG spatial spectral analysis.

[PMID: 42741930] The effect of multimodal sensory therapy in consciousness improvement in patients with acquired brain injury: a systematic review and meta-analysis.

<h4>Purpose</h4>Adults with severe or profound acquired brain injury (ABI) frequently develop disorders of consciousness (DoC). Multimodal sensory therapy (MST) has been proposed to promote arousal and recovery, but effect estimates vary. This review evaluated MST for consciousness outcomes in adults with ABI-related DoC.<h4>Materials and methods</h4>We searched MEDLINE, Embase, Web of Science, CENTRAL, and CINAHL from inception to 28 October 2025 for studies enrolling adults with ABI-related DoC and comparing MST with usual care, sham, or no stimulation. Outcomes were consciousness scores and coma emergence. Risk of bias was assessed using RoB 2 or ROBINS-I. Random-effects models calculated standardized mean differences (SMDs) and odds ratios (ORs).<h4>Results</h4>Eighteen studies with 20 comparisons and 884 participants were included. MST improved consciousness levels versus control (SMD, 1.48; 95% CI, 1.04-1.92). Effects were more consistent with CRS-R assessments (SMD, 1.23; 95% CI, 0.55-1.91). MST was associated with higher odds of coma emergence (OR, 9.85; 95% CI, 3.86-25.01). Risk-of-bias concerns were identified in most studies.<h4>Conclusions</h4>MST was associated with improved consciousness levels and increased probability of coma emergence in adults with ABI-related DoC, but evidence is limited by heterogeneity and methodological limitations. Future trials should use standardized protocols and validated assessments.<h4>Trial registration</h4>The protocol for this study was prospectively registered in the International Prospective Register of Systematic Reviews (CRD420251177793).

[PMID: 42829016] HD-tACS at 40 Hz improves consciousness and brain connectivity in prolonged disorders of consciousness: a randomized sham-controlled clinical trial.

<h4>Background</h4>Frequency-specific high-definition transcranial alternating current stimulation (HD-tACS) may facilitate recovery in patients with prolonged disorders of consciousness (pDoC), but predictive biomarkers and mechanistic insights are limited.<h4>Objective</h4>To investigate the therapeutic efficacy of 40 Hz or 4 Hz HD-tACS in pDoC.<h4>Methods</h4>Sixty pDoC patients were randomized into four groups receiving 40 Hz or 4 Hz HD-tACS, each with a corresponding sham condition. [PMID: 42772877] Apomorphine and psilocybin to improve recovery from coma in the intensive care unit: protocol for a prospective, open-label, dose-escalation study.

The study is considered to involve relatively low risk with the potential to improve future treatment options for DoC patients. If psilocybin and apomorphine are shown to be safe, this would justify a larger multicentre randomised trial to evaluate clinical efficacy. Findings will be disseminated through peer-reviewed journals and scientific conferences.<h4>Trial registration number</h4>2023-503617-30-02.

[PMID: ] Simultaneous metabolic–functional coupling topology in disorders of consciousness

<title>Abstract</title>  <p>  Consciousness depends on coordinated cerebral metabolism and intrinsic brain activity, yet the spatial organization of this neurometabolic coupling across consciousness states remains incompletely understood. We studied 29 patients with prolonged disorders of consciousness (12 MCS, 17 UWS) and 10 preserved-consciousness clinical reference participants using simultaneous PET/MR-derived  <sup>18</sup>  F-FDG PET and resting-state fMRI data. PET-derived Z-standardized FDG uptake and fMRI-derived regional homogeneity (ReHo) and fractional amplitude of low-frequency fluctuations (fALFF) were first compared between MCS and UWS. Cross-modal coupling was then quantified at two levels: within-subject whole-brain spatial coupling and group-level voxel-wise coupling topology. Z-standardized FDG uptake, ReHo, and fALFF showed distinct MCS–UWS difference topographies, whereas whole-brain coupling magnitude did not differ between MCS and UWS for any modality pair. Exploratory voxel-wise analyses identified candidate coupling patterns involving dorsal parietal, posterior midline, occipital, anterior insular, sensorimotor, and cerebellar territories, although none survived FDR or max-statistic FWER correction. These findings do not establish a independently validated clinical biomarker, but suggest that metabolic–functional relationships in prolonged disorders of consciousness may be spatially heterogeneous and not adequately characterized by a single whole-brain coupling measure, providing candidate topographies for future independent replication.  </p>

[PMID: 42710179] Functionalist theories of consciousness: unfolding, substitution and the indeterminacy of function by the physics of the brain.

We consider two recent arguments concerning the question of empirical falsifiability of consciousness theories: the Unfolding Argument (UA) against causal structure theories and the Substitution Argument (SA) according to which if prediction of experience and inference from behaviour are independent, any non-behavioural theory of consciousness is a priori falsified. We argue that the UA is unsound, because the universal approximation theorems don't support it. We then examine whether the SA can be circumvented by "looking inside the brain", i.e., by direct measurements of brain observables. We show that this option is not open to any functionalist theory of consciousness, i.e., any theory that identifies conscious states with some "functional role" (e.g., input-output function, computational role, causal profile) implemented by brain processes. Also, we show that this deeper problem pertains only to functionalist theories. The reason is that functionalism by construction allows for multiple realization of the mental by the physical, and therefore the function identified with consciousness must be defined by some abstract formal structure. But we argue that a theorem first proved by Putnam and recently generalized by Hemmo and Shenker implies that the physics of the brain in its entirety cannot determine uniquely any formal structure: any (complex enough) physical system (e.g., the neural system) implements at once infinitely many formal structures (this is the indeterminacy of function by physics). Finally, we argue that the only way to counter the indeterminacy problem while avoiding behaviourism is to adopt a reductive mind-brain identity theory like Flat Physicalism.

[PMID: 42806264] Binary Classification of Consciousness Using Cerebral Blood Flow and EEG Features.

[PMID: 42829371] Psychedelics and disorders of consciousness: a new frontier in neurotherapeutics.

Disorders of consciousness (DoC), including coma, unresponsive wakefulness syndrome (also known as vegetative state), and minimally conscious state, represent profound disruptions in awareness and responsiveness due to severe brain injury, with limited therapeutic options available. Recent advancements in neuroimaging have improved our ability to detect residual brain activity in DoC patients, but effective interventions remain elusive. Psychedelics like psilocybin, lysergic acid diethylamide (LSD), and N,N-dimethyltryptamine (DMT), which modulate brain network dynamics and enhance neural plasticity, have emerged as candidate pharmacological probes and potential therapeutic strategies for DoC. These substances primarily exert their effects through serotonin 2 A (5-HT<sub>2A</sub>) receptor agonism, inducing widespread changes in cortical activity and connectivity, in contrast to the reduced connectivity typically seen in DoC. The entropic brain hypothesis suggests that psychedelics increase neural entropy, potentially fostering more flexible brain states that could facilitate recovery in DoC patients by restoring communication between isolated brain regions. While direct human feasibility evidence has recently emerged from initial pilot and case studies, controlled clinical evidence demonstrating sustained restoration of consciousness remains absent. Consequently, key challenges such as determining optimal dosing, safety, and ethical considerations must be addressed before clinical application. This perspective reviews the neurobiological mechanisms of psychedelics, their potential for treating DoC, and the clinical and methodological hurdles to overcome, emphasizing the need for interdisciplinary research to translate these ideas into effective therapies. Moreover, this exploration contributes to a deeper understanding of consciousness and its modulation through pharmacological means.

[PMID: ] Hybrid Consciousness and the Cultural Evolution of Leadership

<p>Ninety percent of human societies practise institutionalised alterations of consciousness. Across independent traditions, communities that develop governed protocols for sustained engagement with non-human organisms produce practitioners whose hybrid consciousness becomes the basis of social authority. Drawing on 27 months of apprenticeship-based fieldwork in the Peruvian and Colombian Amazon and comparative analysis of the 2,300-year Buddhist Bodhi tree tradition in Sri Lanka, I argue that inter-species contact protocols function as culturally evolved adaptive technologies that solve a recurrent problem in human social complexity: producing leaders whose authority is corruption-resistant and community-verifiable. The argument carries implications for cultural evolution theory, the study of religion and ritual, and contemporary encounters with non-human intelligence.</p>

[PMID: ] What Artificial Intelligence is Probably Missing—And Why We May Need a New Paradigm: The Ontological Interface Hypothesis

The rapid ascent of Large Language Models (LLMs) has highlighted the possibility that complex syntactic reasoning and semantic manipulation may occur without subjective experience (qualia), raising fundamental questions about the relationship between cognition and consciousness and challenging key assumptions of computational functionalism. This paper introduces the Ontological Interface Hypothesis (OIH), a speculative ontological framework that proposes a triadic distinction between cognition, consciousness, and biological self-preservation. Within this framework, cognition is conceived as an algorithmic, potentially substrate-independent process, while consciousness and biological negentropy are hypothesized to involve non-algorithmic aspects of a more fundamental ontological layer. The thalamocortical complex is tentatively proposed as a phase-synchronized biological interface linking algorithmic processing to this hypothesized layer, rather than as an additional computational engine. Drawing on concepts from information theory, thermodynamics, and the philosophy of mind, the paper explores the potential implications of this framework for understanding the limits of algorithmic explanations of consciousness and life. It also examines functionalist counterarguments and considers whether artificial systems lacking the proposed biological interfaces might differ fundamentally from living organisms in their capacity for subjective experience, self-preservation, and autonomous agency. Ultimately, the framework suggests that the apparent sentience of advanced AI operates as a hermeneutic illusion, demonstrating that while complex syntax can be computationally simulated, genuine meaning and subjective experience likely require a non-algorithmic ontological anchor. The OIH is presented as a conceptual hypothesis whose ontological assumptions and proposed physical mechanisms remain open to further philosophical analysis and empirical investigation.

[PMID: ] The Entangled Loop: Emotion, quantum-like interference and hierarchy in the architecture of consciousness

<p>Why does consciousness feel like something, and why is experience unified rather than fragmented? We propose that these properties arise from an entangled trefoil of emotion, interference-based inference and hierarchical orchestration, shaped by the thermodynamic constraint that living systems must remain far from equilibrium using limited energy. Emotion compresses actions into valenced signals of what matters, while a pleasure cycle schedules competing biological needs through predictive wanting and satiety organised around the true hedonic outcome of liking. Hierarchical orchestrating regions integrate and broadcast information through interference between classical coupled oscillators, shaped by the spectral gaps of the connectome driven by long-range exceptions to local cortical wiring. The architecture of the Entangled Loop consists of three inseparable strands, each continually constraining the other two, with the one operator linking computational possibilities to the arrow of time and energetic cost of running them. Evidence suggests that the same three signatures emerge independently in large language models trained on human language, but as shadows of the generating architecture rather than evidence of consciousness. The trefoil predicts a distinctive human vulnerability because our abundance of rare long-range connections promotes more computation for less energy while making the architecture more fragile. Neuropsychiatric disorders could therefore arise from perturbations entering through any strand and propagating through the others, while the more robust architecture of other species may help explain why animal models reproduce human psychiatric disorders poorly. Sustaining and restoring flexible entanglement in the Entangled Loop may provide mechanistic routes towards flourishing.</p>

Electroencephalography (EEG) provides an accessible approach for assessing functional brain connectivity at the bedside. This study explored the association between EEG connectivity, clinical variables, and 6-month consciousness-related outcomes after brain injury.<h4>Methods</h4>EEG was recorded from 111 hospitalized patients with impaired consciousness after brain injury. Baseline clinical status was assessed using the GCS. Patients were followed for 6 months and categorized according to command-following ability into good outcome and bad outcome groups. Functional connectivity was computed using weighted phase lag index (wPLI), debiased squared wPLI (wPLI<sup>2</sup>), and coherence. Clinical and EEG variables were evaluated using group comparisons, multivariable regression, receiver operating characteristic analysis, leave-one-out cross-validation, and sensitivity analyses.<h4>Results</h4>Patients in the good outcome group were younger and had higher baseline GCS scores than those in the bad outcome group. Among EEG measures, alpha-band wPLI during the moving condition showed the strongest nominal group difference, with higher values in the good outcome group (nominal p = 0.006). This result did not survive false discovery rate correction across EEG connectivity variables (pFDR = 0.169). Alpha-band wPLI showed a nominal adjusted association with the 6-month consciousness-related outcome after adjustment for age and GCS, although the estimate was imprecise and attenuated in sensitivity analyses. In leave-one-out cross-validation, the clinical single-modality analysis using age and GCS achieved an AUC of 0.778, whereas the multimodality analysis combining age, GCS, and mean wPLI achieved an AUC of 0.733. The incremental improvement from adding EEG connectivity to clinical variables was not statistically significant.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Alpha-band wPLI during task-based EEG showed an exploratory association with 6-month consciousness-related outcome after brain injury. However, this finding did not survive correction for multiple EEG comparisons and did not significantly improve discrimination beyond age and GCS in this cohort. Task-related EEG connectivity may serve as a candidate complementary marker, but larger multicenter studies with external validation are needed.
---
[PMID: 42902880] Trigeminal Nerve Stimulation for Disorders of Consciousness: A Narrative Review of Potential Mechanisms, Clinical Evidence, and Future Perspectives.

<h4>Background</h4>Disorders of consciousness (DoC), including the vegetative state/unresponsive wakefulness syndrome (VS/UWS) and the minimally conscious state (MCS), present a major clinical challenge due to limited therapeutic options. Trigeminal nerve stimulation (TNS) has recently emerged as a promising neuromodulatory intervention.<h4>Mechanisms and current evidence</h4>The trigeminal nuclei send efferent fibers to the thalamus and the ascending reticular system, which then broadly innervate multiple brain areas via the ascending reticular activating system (ARAS) through both direct and indirect pathways. Capitalizing on the trigeminal nerve's distinctive anatomy, TNS has been hypothesized to potentially promote consciousness recovery in preclinical models by activating specific pathways, modulating the release of key neurotransmitters (e.g., hypocretin, dopamine, and norepinephrine), and influencing cerebral hemodynamics. Furthermore, randomized controlled trials (RCTs) involving both external TNS (eTNS) and acoustic-electric TNS have highlighted a possible direction for its application as a novel therapy for DoC; the findings appear to suggest potential clinical relevance, although current evidence remains constrained by small samples, patient heterogeneity, variable stimulation protocols, and limited long-term follow-up.<h4>Challenges</h4>The clinical translation of TNS, however, faces pressing obstacles, including uncertainty regarding optimal stimulation targets and parameters, insufficient long-term safety and efficacy data, incompletely elucidated mechanisms of action, and a lack of suitable preclinical models.<h4>Conclusions</h4>TNS may represent a promising therapeutic approach for DoC, but further high-quality studies are needed to establish its mechanisms, safety, efficacy, and optimal stimulation strategies.

[PMID: 42853770] The Weight of the Real: A Phenomenological Exploration of the "Excess of Reality" in Borderline Consciousness Through a Comparison with Schizophrenia.

<h4>Background</h4>Phenomenological research has long provided a foundation for understanding the schizophrenic condition as a whole, yet its contribution to borderline personality disorder (BPD) remains comparatively scattered across different lines of inquiry. Existing phenomenological studies on BPD typically focus on fragmented lived temporality, dysphoric mood, emotion dysregulation and anomalies of lived body and interpersonal experience, often overlooking the affective and sensory impact of a reality experienced as independent of and indifferent to consciousness. To address this imbalance, this paper uses ''phenomenological realism'' to examine BPD psychopathology as an integrated whole, framing this unique feature of borderline self- and world-experience through the concept of ''ontic gravity.'' Summary: The borderline mode of consciousness, especially in states of acute distress, is characterized in terms of an "excess of reality". By comparing BPD with schizophrenia, the authors highlight a fundamental difference in derealization: while the schizophrenic experience is marked by an "evanescence of reality" and a search for hidden meaning, including one's role in its constitution, the borderline experience involves a traumatic, blunt, and intrusive concreteness. Using the concept of the phenomenological epoché, the paper argues that while schizophrenia resembles an involuntary suspension of the natural attitude, BPD can be understood as a total immersion in it, where the person is unable to distance themselves from what is immediately given as fact.<h4>Key message</h4>The tendency among persons with BPD to feel "crushed" by events during severe distress may be associated with a difficulty in taking a "transcendental" attitude. Unlike the "oblique gaze" of people with schizophrenia that searches for how meaning is constituted, the borderline "direct gaze" tends to experience bodily feelings, encounters with others, and perceptions as raw, physically abrasive facts that imply unmediated suffering rather than reflection.

[PMID: ] Why Testing Structural Theories Requires the Unconscious Condition

<p>Consciousness science has traditionally proceeded by contrasting neural activity when a stimulus is consciously perceived against activity when the same stimulus is not perceived. This contrastive approach is well suited to locating neural correlates of consciousness, but it says little about why an experience has the particular character it does. A recent structural turn addresses this gap by comparing the relational structure of qualia with the relational structure of a candidate physical substrate. We review the main structure-matching programmes and what they offer beyond the contrastive approach. We then argue that all of them rest on an assumption that has never been tested directly, which we call the structural exclusivity assumption: That a neural structure matching the qualia structure reflects the neural substrate of consciousness itself, rather than merely the processing of information that can occur without conscious experience. The assumption is empirical rather than philosophical, and the standard structure-matching design cannot test it, because qualia and physical structures are estimated while the content is consciously perceived. We set out the design that would test it, and review the psychophysical and neural evidence currently bearing on it. That evidence consistently suggests that relational structure survives absence of awareness, though no existing study settles the question. We argue that structure-matching and contrastive designs must be combined, and consider what follows for the selection of candidate structures and for the promise of the dissolution of the hard problem of consciousness.</p>

[PMID: ] What is it like to be a blob? A distributed, decentred and context-sensitive neurobiology of consciousness

Consciousness is often studied by asking whether particular brain regions or systems are privileged. We tested a distributed, decentred and context-sensitive alternative using 579 consciousness-focused neuroimaging studies. Semantic clustering of titles and abstracts identified seven domains whose corrected meta-analytic maps showed limited overlap and no invariant cross-domain core. These maps occupied 18.0% of grey matter while extending across 91.0% of brain regions. After controlling for reporting topography, distributed cognitive, sensorimotor and organismic-affective maps reconstructed the residual consciousness landscape better than spatial surrogates. Organismic-affective maps contributed most, but functional contributions varied contextually across domains. Theory-associated regional loci added no additional meaningful prediction. Information carried by the functional families was both redundant and synergistic. Coactivation-network and hypergraph analyses revealed a shared network repertoire but context-dependent higher-order coalitions. These results argue against an invariant core, supporting a distributed neurobiology of consciousness in which specialised systems are weighted and combined differently across contexts.

[PMID: 42682893] Implantable vagus nerve stimulation for the treatment of disorders of consciousness with epilepsy: a retrospective propensity score-matched clinical study.

<h4>Background</h4>Disorders of consciousness (DoC) have limited effective therapies. Implantable vagus nerve stimulation (VNS) is established for epilepsy but remains underexplored for DoC with comorbid epilepsy.<h4>Objectives</h4>To evaluate whether VNS is associated with improved consciousness recovery in DoC patients with epilepsy and to explore factors related to response.<h4>Design</h4>Retrospective propensity score-matched cohort study.<h4>Methods</h4>Ninety DoC patients with epilepsy received implantable VNS or conservative management. The primary outcome was clinically meaningful improvement at 1 year (Coma Recovery Scale-Revised (CRS-R) increase ⩾3 points). Propensity score matching (1:1) balanced baseline characteristics (23 per group). Longitudinal CRS-R trajectories were assessed using linear mixed-effects models with Type III ANOVA. Logistic regression within the VNS cohort explored factors associated with responder status; adverse events were extracted from operative and follow-up records.<h4>Results</h4>The 1-year responder rate was higher with VNS than with conservative management (34.78% vs 4.35%; two-sided Fisher's exact test, <i>p</i> = 0.022). Longitudinal analyses showed a significant group × time interaction (χ<sup>2</sup> = 43.535, <i>p</i> < 0.001) with greater CRS-R gains from 3 months onward in the VNS group. Within the VNS cohort, responder status was associated with baseline minimally conscious state (aOR = 9.750, 95% confidence interval (CI) 1.592-59.695; <i>p</i> = 0.014) and better seizure control (McHugh classification; aOR = 22.667, 95% CI 3.140-163.629; <i>p</i> = 0.002). Traumatic etiology was not associated with 12-month net CRS-R improvement after adjustment for baseline CRS-R (β = 0.893, 95% CI -1.583 to 3.369; <i>p</i> = 0.466). Five patients reported stimulation-related hoarseness/dysphonia, and two had surgical-site complications; no device removal occurred.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Implantable VNS was associated with higher 1-year clinically meaningful improvement and greater longitudinal CRS-R gains than conservative management in DoC patients with epilepsy. Prospective controlled studies are warranted.

[PMID: 42882108] Rehabilitation in Pediatric Patients with Acute and Subacute Acquired Brain Injury: A Scoping Review on Non-Pharmacological Interventions.

<b>Background:</b> Acquired brain injury in children and adolescents may lead to long-term neurological, cognitive, motor, and behavioral impairments. However, pediatric-specific evidence on rehabilitation strategies remains limited. <b>Objective:</b> This review aimed to summarize current evidence on non-pharmacological neurorehabilitation interventions for children and adolescents with acute/subacute acquired or traumatic brain injury, including a subset of patients with disorders of consciousness. <b>Methods:</b> A literature search was conducted in PubMed, Embase, Web of Science, and Scopus. Eligible studies investigated non-pharmacological rehabilitation approaches in pediatric or adolescent patients with severe acquired brain injury, traumatic brain injury, stroke, or disorders of consciousness. Data on interventions, outcomes, and main findings were extracted. <b>Results:</b> The reviewed studies included heterogeneous interventions, such as multidisciplinary rehabilitation, constraint-induced movement therapy, virtual-reality-based training, speech therapy, parent-child interaction therapy, sensory stimulation, autonomic monitoring, and neuromodulation. Overall, these approaches were feasible and generally well tolerated, with reported improvements in motor, functional, behavioral, autonomic, and consciousness-related outcomes. <b>Conclusions:</b> Non-pharmacological rehabilitation may support recovery in children and adolescents with severe brain injury, although current evidence remains preliminary and heterogeneous. Further multicenter studies with standardized protocols and outcome measures are needed to guide pediatric-specific rehabilitation practice.

[PMID: ] The Domain Loop Model: Grounding Consciousness Science in Whole-Body Homeostatic Computation

<p>Contemporary theories of consciousness—including Integrated Information Theory, Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought theories, and active inference frameworks—have advanced the modelling of conscious experience. Although some frameworks are substrate-neutral in principle, empirical research across these approaches predominantly focuses on neural computation, often measuring brain activity while conceptualising the body primarily as a source of sensory input rather than as a constitutive substrate. This focus neglects a key biological reality: consciousness arises from organisms composed of trillions of cells engaged in continuous homeostatic computation, with neurons representing only a small fraction of this information-processing architecture (in humans, neurons constitute less than 1% of total cellular computation). The Domain Loop Model addresses this gap, proposing that consciousness emerges from three interconnected domains: the interoceptive domain (whole-body homeostatic computation), the exteroceptive domain (environmental information processed through predictive perception), and the narrator domain (the integration of internal state with external context, constituting conscious experience). These domains are hierarchically organised, with interoceptive signals carrying evolutionary priority due to the existential consequences of homeostatic failure, creating a fundamental asymmetry in conscious experience. Drawing on clinical observations of chronic anxiety (Hartshorn &amp; O'Brien, 2024) and empirical research on interoceptive processing, the model elucidates: (1) how unconscious prioritization of interoceptive signals creates vulnerabilities in precision-weighting systems; (2) how interoceptive opacity—the inaccessibility of most bodily computation to conscious awareness—permits pathological states to persist; (3) how epigenetic mechanisms encode precision weights across timescales from days to decades; and (4) how consciousness evolves through biological encounters that physically reorganize the computational substrate. The Domain Loop Model accounts for phenomena that current theories struggle to explain, including the persistence of chronic anxiety despite uncertainty reduction, enduring consciousness changes following post-viral syndromes, and biological substrates underlying individual differences in anxiety vulnerability. This framework suggests research directions measuring immune, metabolic, epigenetic, and microbiome markers alongside neural activity, and supports clinical applications integrating interventions targeting both physiological dysregulation and conscious interpretation patterns. The Domain Loop Model further aligns consciousness with evolutionary processes through transgenerational epigenetic inheritance, operating across timescales from acute homeostatic shifts to generational inheritance.</p>

[PMID: ] Digital Mediation, Selfhood, and Hybrid Agency: A Minimal Three-Dimensional Framework

<title>Abstract</title>  <p>Claims that digital technologies blur the boundaries of consciousness and personal identity often collapse several distinct philosophical questions. This article develops a minimal three-dimensional framework of digital mediation that distinguishes cognitive integration, self-representational mediation, and agential distribution. Unlike existing multidimensional accounts that remain within one domain, the framework coordinates evidence across cognition, selfhood, and action while specifying which cross-domain inferences require an additional bridge argument. Digital artifacts may scaffold or partly constitute cognitive processes without extending phenomenal consciousness; profiles, avatars, and technologically organized memories may participate in narrative and practical selfhood without multiplying numerically distinct persons; and artificial systems may redistribute control within hybrid action without acquiring moral responsibility or personhood. Each dimension is assessed through explicit ordinal criteria rather than an impressionistic low–high scale. The framework is applied to conversational artificial intelligence, personal-memory systems, immersive avatars, recommender systems, and brain–computer interfaces. It also derives dimension-specific vulnerabilities and safeguards: continuity and provenance for cognitive dependence, contestability and contextual integrity for mediated selfhood, and meaningful control and responsibility mapping for distributed action. The result is a relational account of digital transformation that identifies how technologies reorganize thinking, self-interpretation, and control without positing a unitary “digital consciousness.”</p>

[PMID: ] The perceived role of consciousness in moral status attributions

<p>Determining which entities deserve moral consideration is crucial to policy debates about animal welfare and artificial intelligence regulation. A common assumption is that consciousness is a key criterion for moral status, often considered to be necessary or sufficient for it. However, it is unclear whether public attitudes also share this assumption. In a large, preregistered study (N=1493), we tested the perceived relationship between consciousness and moral status by triangulating direct questions, moral dilemmas, and attribution questions across diverse entities. Although consciousness was strongly associated with moral status, it was neither perceived as necessary nor sufficient: participants attributed moral status without consciousness, and denied moral status despite its presence. Moreover, respondents clustered into distinct decision profiles, which were predicted by their individual background. Therefore, moral decisions did not depend solely on the properties of the target entity, but also on those of the person making the judgment. These results have implications for how ethical and policy decisions are framed.</p>

[PMID: ] The Ontological Interface Hypothesis: A Triadic Framework for Consciousness, Biological Negentropy, and the Entropic Limits of Algorithmic AI

The rapid ascent of Large Language Models (LLMs) has empirically demonstrated that complex syntactic reasoning and semantic manipulation do not require subjective experience (qualia). This paper proposes the Ontological Interface Hypothesis (OIH), a triadic framework that decouples cognition, consciousness, and biological self-preservation. We argue that while cognition is an algorithmic, substrate-independent process susceptible to the Data Processing Inequality and entropic degradation (Model Collapse), consciousness and biological negentropy may involve non-algorithmic aspects of a more fundamental ontological layer. We model the thalamocortical complex not as an additional computational engine, but as a phase-synchronized biological interface connecting the algorithmic substrate to this deeper realm. Furthermore, we address standard functionalist counter-arguments, suggesting that artificial agents lacking such an interface are likely to remain prone to cognitive drift and physical degradation, functioning primarily as deterministic proxies of human intent rather than autonomous subjective entities.

[PMID: ] Consciousness and emotions in non-human animals: Are laypeople more or less skeptical than experts?

<p>Consciousness and the capacity to experience affective states, such as pain, pleasure, fear, and hunger (i.e., sentience) are seen as key determinants of an individual’s moral status, a view that is shared by scientists, philosophers, and laypeople. Whether (and which) non-human animals are believed to possess these capacities can therefore have important consequences for how they are treated. In this registered report (study design registration: https://doi.org/10.71240/lcyc.238674), we measured people’s attributions of consciousness and emotions to a diverse set of animals, testing whether laypeople overattribute (as suggested by research on anthropomorphizing) or underattribute these mental capacities to animals (as suggested by research on motivated mind denial). Animal consciousness and emotions are famously challenging to study. In the absence of an unambiguous benchmark against which people’s beliefs could be compared, we tested whether laypeople, a nationally representative sample of 600 U.S. participants, are more or less skeptical about animal consciousness and emotions than scientific experts, a sample of 100 behavioral ecologists, evolutionary biologists, and other animal researchers. We find that, compared to scientific experts, laypeople were less likely to attribute consciousness (-8 percentage points), and substantially less likely to attribute emotions (-21 percentage points) to non-human animals. Laypeople were on average more confident in their beliefs than scientific experts.</p>

[PMID: 42669846] Dynamic Global and Local Enhancement of Theta-Band Functional Networks as Neural Markers of Spinal Cord Stimulation Efficacy in Disorders of Consciousness.

<h4>Background</h4>Spinal cord stimulation (SCS) has emerged as a promising intervention for patients with disorders of consciousness (DoC). However, clinical optimization is hindered by a lack of interpretable prognostic biomarkers, and the neural mechanisms associated with recovery after SCS remain largely elusive.<h4>Methods</h4>Nineteen DoC patients undergoing 70 Hz SCS were enrolled and categorized into effective (n = 9) and ineffective (n = 10) groups based on longitudinal clinical improvements. Resting-state electroencephalography (EEG) was recorded across baseline, intra-stimulation, and poststimulation phases. To investigate neural dynamics, theta-band dynamic functional connectivity (dFC) was analyzed using the weighted phase lag index (wPLI) combined with a sliding window approach. Global and local dFC metrics, alongside region-specific connectivity strengths, were quantified to characterize the global-local collaborative mechanisms induced by SCS.<h4>Results</h4>Compared with the ineffective group, the effective group demonstrated enhanced global integration and decoupled regional segregation under SCS, evidenced by synchronized reduction in global average path length and suppression in local clustering (p<sub>TPL</sub> = 0.0090; p<sub>TLE</sub> = 0.0090; p<sub>TCC</sub> = 0.0378). Regionally, increased functional connectivity was primarily observed in the right temporal cortex (p = 0.0054), whereas decreased connectivity was identified in the left occipital region (p = 0.0218). Furthermore, dynamic EEG features were significantly correlated with 3-month CRS-R improvement scores (p < 0.05).<h4>Conclusion</h4>This dynamic global and local enhancement in the theta-band dFC serves as an interpretable neural marker for predicting SCS efficacy. These findings offer novel insights into the system-level collaborative mechanisms essential for consciousness restoration.<h4>Trial registration</h4>Chinese Clinical Trial Registry: ChiCTR2200061278.

[PMID: 42787178] Median effective dose of fospropofol disodium for suppression of the cough reflex during tracheostomy tube exchange in patients with disorders of consciousness receiving fixed-dose sufentanil.

<h4>Objective</h4>The aim of this study was to determine the median effective dose (ED<sub>50</sub>) and 95% effective dose (ED<sub>95</sub>) of fospropofol disodium required to suppress the cough response during tracheostomy tube exchange in patients with disorders of consciousness receiving a background dose of sufentanil 5 μg/kg.<h4>Methods</h4>Twenty-nine patients with disorders of consciousness requiring general anesthesia for surgical procedures were enrolled. All had a pre-existing tracheostomy and were classified as ASA IV. General anesthesia was induced with intravenous sufentanil 5 μg/kg, followed by administration of fospropofol disodium using a sequential allocation design. A cough response graded as III or higher was defined as a positive response. The study was terminated after the occurrence of 10 effective crossover points. Probit regression analysis was used to estimate the ED<sub>50</sub>, ED<sub>95</sub>, and corresponding 95% confidence intervals. Mean arterial pressure (MAP), heart rate (HR), and respiratory rate (RR) was recorded before anesthesia induction (T0), before tracheostomy tube exchange (T1), and immediately after tube exchange (T2). The incidences of hypotension and apnea were also recorded.<h4>Results</h4>The ED<sub>50</sub> and ED<sub>95</sub> of fospropofol disodium for suppression of the cough response were 9.41 mg/kg (95% CI, 8.08-10.64 mg/kg) and 12.66 mg/kg (95% CI, 11.01-26.05 mg/kg), respectively. Repeated-measures analysis of variance demonstrated significant time-dependent effects on MAP and RR (both p < 0.001), whereas HR demonstrated no significant changes over time (p > 0.05). Compared with T0, MAP and RR were significantly reduced at T1 and T2 (both p < 0.01), with no statistically significant differences observed between T1 and T2 (both p > 0.05). The incidence rates of hypotension, apnea, and oxygen desaturation were higher in the 12 mg/kg group than in the 10 mg/kg group (66.67% vs. 15.38%, 50.00% vs. 7.69%, and 16.67% vs. 0%, respectively).<h4>Conclusion</h4>In the presence of sufentanil 5 μg/kg, the ED<sub>50</sub> and ED<sub>95</sub> of fospropofol disodium for suppressing the cough reflex during tracheostomy tube exchange in patients with disorders of consciousness were 9.41 mg/kg (95% CI, 8.08-10.64 mg/kg) and 12.66 mg/kg (95% CI, 11.01-26.05 mg/kg), respectively. The suppressive effect was dose-dependent; however, higher doses were associated with increased risks of hypotension and apnea, indicating the need for individualized dose titration.

[PMID: ] Digital Transformation and Environmental Consciousness: Panel Data Evidence from EU E-Commerce Markets

<title>Abstract</title>  <p>The COVID-19 pandemic has dramatically accelerated digital transformation globally, fundamentally altering e-commerce adoption patterns across different economies. This study investigates the determinants of e-commerce intensity — the percentage of total enterprise turnover derived from electronic commerce — across eight European Union countries from 2014 to 2023. We focus particularly on environmental consciousness factors and the structural break caused by COVID-19. Using a balanced panel dataset and advanced econometric techniques including two-way fixed effects, common correlated effects estimation, and threshold regression, we examine how digital infrastructure, environmental factors, logistics performance, and economic conditions influence e-commerce penetration at the national level. Our results reveal several significant findings. First, internet penetration exhibits threshold effects with stronger impacts below 89.45% penetration. Second, environmental consciousness, proxied by CO₂ emissions per capita, negatively affects e-commerce adoption (β = -1.432, p < 0.01). Third, we document a "logistics performance paradox" where better traditional logistics infrastructure is associated with lower e-commerce intensity (β = -8.954, p < 0.05). Fourth, COVID-19 caused a permanent structural break, increasing e-commerce intensity by an average of 2.31 percentage points across countries. Fifth, significant heterogeneity exists across countries, with Ireland and Sweden leading adoption rates while France lags considerably. Policy simulations indicate that combined environmental and digital infrastructure policies yield the highest returns (+ 5.73 percentage points), while traditional logistics investments may be counterproductive. These findings have important implications for digital transformation strategies, suggesting that environmental and digital policies should be integrated and that conventional assumptions about logistics infrastructure prerequisites may need revision. This research contributes to the digital transformation literature by providing the first comprehensive panel analysis linking environmental consciousness to e-commerce adoption and by quantifying the permanent effects of the COVID-19 structural break on digital commerce patterns.</p>

[PMID: ] Transient Anisocoria and Sudden Decrease in Consciousness Following Thoracic Epidural Analgesia: A Case Report

<title>Abstract</title>  <p>  <bold>Background:</bold>  Anisocoria during neuraxial anesthesia is an uncommon finding that may represent transient autonomic dysfunction or serious neurological complications, posing a diagnostic challenge for anesthesiologists.  <bold>Case Presentation:</bold>  A previously healthy 33-year-old woman underwent elective breast implant exchange and liposuction with planned thoracic epidural analgesia combined with general anesthesia. Shortly after administration of 4 mL of an epidural solution containing levobupivacaine and morphine, she developed abrupt alteration of consciousness and anisocoria, characterized by left-sided mydriasis and right-sided miosis. Despite the neurological event, she remained hemodynamically stable and showed no signs of seizures, respiratory compromise, or cardiovascular collapse. Airway protection was promptly achieved with tracheal intubation and controlled ventilation. Serial pupillary and cardiorespiratory monitoring demonstrated complete spontaneous resolution of anisocoria approximately 60 minutes later. The postoperative course was uneventful, with no neurological deficits or recurrence of symptoms during 36 hours of observation.  <bold>Conclusion:</bold>  Transient anisocoria associated with altered consciousness following epidural analgesia is rare and may be related to extensive cephalad neuraxial spread and transient sympathetic dysfunction. Early recognition, airway protection when indicated, and close neurological monitoring are essential for favorable outcomes.  </p>

[PMID: ] Focused Ultrasound Neuromodulation of the Central Lateral Thalamus in a Non-Human Primate Model of Disorders of Consciousness

<h4>Objectives</h4>  Disorders of consciousness (DOC) affect an estimated 200,000 patients in the United States. Effective treatment options have remained elusive. Focused ultrasound neuromodulation (FUS) of brain regions controlling consciousness, such as the central lateral thalamus (CLT), has emerged as a promising non-invasive treatment. However, prior studies have been limited by broad effective stimulation volumes, missing control conditions, and limited target validation. This study investigated the effects of FUS of the CLT in a nonhuman primate model of DOC with robust control conditions and more robust targeting than previous experiments. <h4>Materials and Methods</h4>  Two male rhesus macaques underwent 1-hour propofol anesthesia sessions as a model of DOC. The largest observed effect was increased body movement under the unfocused paradigm (  r rb = +0.58), though this did not reach statistical significance. Animals remained physiologically stable throughout sessions and recovery periods, with no clinically significant changes in heart rate, breathing rate, blood oxygen saturation, or other safety concerns.  <h4>Conclusions</h4>  Under the tested parameters, FUS of the CLT did not produce measurable changes in arousal. The absence of adverse physiological effects supports the safety of this FUS paradigm. The largest effect occurring under spatially distributed sonication, consistent with prior evidence that broader stimulation volumes produce stronger arousal responses, suggests that distributed network stimulation may warrant investigation as an alternative to focal targeting. Future studies should investigate this possibility and should include robust control conditions to properly quantify effects.

[PMID: ] Brain dynamics exhibit scale-dependent reversibility during consciousness transitions

Consciousness is a dynamic brain state, yet the systems-level mechanisms underlying transitions into and out of unconsciousness remain poorly understood. It is unclear whether neural dynamics during loss of consciousness (LOC) and recovery of consciousness (ROC) simply retrace the same trajectory or instead follow distinct paths across multiple spatial scales. Here, we simultaneously measured local electrophysiology, whole-brain functional MRI, and pupil dynamics in rats during graded propofol anesthesia to characterize consciousness transitions from local circuits to whole-brain networks. We found that local field potential, regional BOLD responses, and pairwise functional connectivity exhibited largely reversible changes between LOC and ROC. In contrast, the global brain organization showed distinct and asymmetric patterns during the two transitions, as consistently revealed by traveling-wave propagation, low-dimensional network trajectories, and graph-theoretical analyses. Importantly, brain-wide coupling between pupil dynamics and regional BOLD activity remained highly consistent during LOC and ROC, indicating that these distinct global trajectories cannot be simply explained by differences in neuromodulatory tone. Together, our findings identify scale-dependent reversibility as a systems-level organizing principle of consciousness transitions. These results suggest that recovery of consciousness is an active process of large-scale network reorganization rather than merely the reversal of anesthetic suppression.

[PMID: 42744134] The role of stimulus-induced skin conductance in disorder of consciousness: A systematic review.

Disorders of consciousness (DoC) are characterized by severe impairments in arousal or awareness and remain challenging to assess accurately with standard behavioral tools alone. Skin conductance (SC), as an index of autonomic arousal, may offer a complementary, motor-independent marker of residual responsiveness. This systematic review examined studies combining sensory stimulation paradigms with SC recordings in patients with DoC, aiming to identify which stimuli are most frequently employed and how they relate to clinical and neurophysiological outcomes. A comprehensive search of PubMed, Scopus, EBSCO, Embase, Cochrane Database, and Web of Science yielded to nine eligible studies, comprising a total of 134 patients diagnosed with coma, vegetative state/unresponsive wakefulness syndrome, or minimally conscious state. Emotionally salient or personally relevant stimuli tended to elicit stronger SC responses than neutral stimulation, although responses in patients were often delayed, attenuated, or inconsistent compared with healthy controls. From a neurobiological perspective, reproducible SC differentiation between emotional and neutral stimuli may reflect residual engagement of limbic-autonomic and salience-related networks, rather than a simple spinal or brainstem reflex. Accordingly, SC should not be considered a standalone diagnostic marker, but a low-cost, bedside-compatible complement to behavioural scales that may help identify covert autonomic processing and guide standardized, multimodal assessment protocols, particularly those based on validated affective environmental sounds.

[PMID: 42746071] From chemistry to cognition: the adaptive origins of consciousness under constraint.

Consciousness is often treated as an evolutionary anomaly, a costly capacity with unclear adaptive origins. Here, cognition and consciousness are distinguished as separate evolutionary lineages, with consciousness arising when an organism must coordinate otherwise distinct regulatory processes to resolve competing demands affecting its own viability. Its functional precursors predate nervous systems, emerging first in boundary-preserving chemical and metabolic regulation. An evolutionary ladder is developed in which proto-control preserves boundaries by selectively permitting, inhibiting, and localizing destruction; meta-control arbitrates among competing local controllers; and settlement control resolves conflict by making competing regulatory demands comparable and jointly binding for policy selection. It follows that artificial consciousness will not emerge from greater cognitive sophistication alone; it will require self-maintaining systems that settle competing demands under real constraint. The framework yields six falsifiable predictions spanning comparative biology, neuroscience, and artificial intelligence. On this view, consciousness is not evolution's anomaly but its solution: self-anchored, cross-domain settlement under constraint.

Multimodal hybrid systems and artificial intelligence-enhanced decoding algorithms represent the primary trajectories for future clinical translation. [PMID: 42816729] Learning in plants: A conceptual and experimental primer.

Whether plants are capable of associative learning remains a contested question, often complicated by conceptual ambiguity and methodological limitations. This article provides a critical and constructive primer on how to investigate associative learning in plants within a rigorous experimental framework. We begin by clarifying the concept of associative learning and distinguishing it from related phenomena such as habituation, sensitisation, and pseudo-conditioning. We then identify a set of minimal behavioural prerequisites that any system must satisfy to support associative processes. Evidence from plant biology indicates that plants possess these prerequisite capacities across multiple temporal and organisational scales. We further review historical and recent experimental attempts to demonstrate associative learning in plants, highlighting recurring methodological limitations. Building on principles from the experimental analysis of behaviour, we outline key design requirements for plant-conditioning studies, including identifying appropriate unconditioned stimulus-response (US-UR) complexes, operationalising conditioned stimuli, and implementing controls to rule out non-associative explanations. As we argue, adequately controlled experiments have not yet provided robust evidence for associative learning in plants, leaving open whether these capacities are integrated in ways that enable plants to form learned associations. By integrating behavioural theory with advances in plant physiology and imaging, future research can move beyond suggestive findings toward decisive tests. More broadly, this work contributes to a functionally grounded understanding of learning that does not presuppose a nervous system, thereby expanding the comparative scope of cognition across living systems.

[PMID: ] From synergy to redundancy: An information-relation account of consciousness

<p>Research on the neural basis of consciousness has largely focused either on where and when neural activity represents conscious contents, or on the interactions through which distributed neural populations become integrated. We argue that these approaches can be connected by analysing information relations among neural representations. A perceptual content can be synergistic when it is carried by the joint state of multiple neural sources, or redundant when overlapping information about that content is shared across sources. We propose that these information-relations provide a common language for major theories of consciousness, linking irreducibility, recurrence, and global availability. Recent evidence suggests that conscious contents may be constructed through synergy-dominant interactions and subsequently stabilized through redundancy-dominant representations, with specific contributions by aperiodic and oscillatory activity. Together, this account reframes conscious content as a dynamically organized information structure, in which synergistic interactions construct nonseparable representations, and redundancy makes those representations stably shared across the system, thereby providing a common framework and an information-theoretical axis for relating major theories of consciousness.</p>

[PMID: 42904947] Some Twitch chats exhibit multifractal characteristics resembling stream-of-consciousness narrative.

Live-streaming platforms generate high-frequency records of collective human activity in which many loosely coordinated users react in real time to shared external stimuli and to one another. We investigate whether the temporal organization of Twitch chat messages exhibits multifractal signatures characteristic of complex dynamical systems. Selected high-activity chats were converted into time series by measuring the length of each consecutive message in words, with platform emotes treated as individual tokens. The resulting signals display heterogeneous fluctuations, bursty organization, and heavy-tailed distributions that, in several cases, are better captured by q-Weibull forms than by conventional light-tailed models. Autocorrelation analysis reveals persistent temporal dependence over broad ranges of message lags, indicating that the variability is not produced by independent message generation alone. Multifractal Detrended Fluctuation Analysis shows that some chats possess well-developed multifractal spectra and generalized Hurst exponents strongly dependent on moment order, whereas others exhibit weak scaling, approximately monofractal behavior, or no well-defined scaling regime. Surrogate tests indicate that temporal correlations are essential for the observed multifractality, while heavy tails enhance its apparent strength. Under particular dynamical conditions, Twitch chat may, therefore, form an emergent collective signal whose fragmented, reactive, associative, and multiscale organization is structurally reminiscent of stream-of-consciousness narrative. This expression is used strictly as a structural and dynamical analogy, not as a claim of a unified collective mind or of semantic, cognitive, or phenomenological equivalence with literary stream-of-consciousness writing. The results position live-stream chat dynamics within the broader physics of complex, intermittent, human-generated signals.

[PMID: 42571580] Integrated information theory (IIT) and the testability of the silent neuron predictions.

Integrated information theory (IIT) makes two predictions about the role of inactive neurons in consciousness. According to the silent brain (SB) prediction, rendering all active neurons inactive ("silent") in the physical substrate of consciousness (the "main complex") does not eliminate the presence of consciousness, because the neurons are still able to spike. According to the disabled neuron (DN) prediction, rendering a <i>subset</i> of silent neurons in the main complex no longer able to spike ("disabled") can impact the qualitative character of experiences "nonconventionally" associated with those neurons. Bartlett (2022) argues that these predictions are untestable, because evidence for either prediction would imply that the testing conditions were not met. In this paper, we provide a detailed analysis of both silent neuron predictions, showing how they can in fact be tested. For the SB case, we clarify how a neural mechanism <i>outside</i> of the main complex can yield the required report of consciousness while maintaining the SB state. For the DN case, we distinguish between two ways of explaining how a neural mechanism could casually interact with the main complex: an IIT-inspired "dispositionalist" explanation, and a more conventional "actualist" explanation. Drawing on the work of Imre Lakatos, we conclude with a discussion of how the distinction between the two explanations sheds light on why it is so difficult to resolve theoretical disputes about consciousness. Despite these difficulties, we provide a framework that can lead to concrete progress for consciousness science.

[PMID: ] Systematic theory–method dependencies in consciousness research

<p>Scientific theories of consciousness are typically evaluated by comparing empirical findings across experimental paradigms. Such comparisons implicitly assume that different paradigms provide commensurable evidence and do not systematically favour particular theoretical interpretations. Here, we test this assumption using the updated ConTraSt database, comprising 518 published consciousness experiments, and Bayesian regression models relating methodological characteristics to theoretical interpretations. We find decisive evidence that experimental methodology predicts whether studies support, challenge, or do not mention major theories of consciousness. These associations are robust across multiple Bayesian model comparison approaches and remain evident across a range of prior specifications. Methodological effects differ substantially across theories, indicating that theories occupy partially distinct methodological niches rather than a common empirical space. While these findings do not imply that any individual theory is more or less valid, they suggest that empirical support is systematically shaped by methodological tradition. Ultimately, it can be challenged if neural correlates of consciousness are “studying the same things” – and if not, what they in that case are actually about. Consequently, direct comparisons between theories may be more constrained than generally assumed. Our results highlight the need for greater methodological diversity in theory testing and suggest that future progress in consciousness science may require approaches that explicitly address the relationship between experimental methodology and theoretical interpretation.</p>

[PMID: 42565836] From male caduco to altered consciousness: two Medici recipes through the lens of modern consciousness science.

Together, they provide a case study of how early modern medicine approached sudden, reversible episodes of impaired awareness and responsiveness, phenomena that modern neurology situates at the intersection of epileptology and consciousness science. Drawing on archival sources from the Archivio di Stato di Firenze, this paper examines the language, ingredients, and stated uses of these preparations and interprets them through the modern distinction between the level of consciousness and its content. This study argues that the two recipes reflect an undifferentiated therapeutic practice directed at functions that contemporary consciousness science would distinguish, and that the empirical observation and attempted reversal of transient losses of responsiveness long preceded any mechanistic account of such phenomena. Their circulation through Anna Maria Luisa's courtly and epistolary networks helped sustain her political relevance during the dissolution of the Medici dynasty and highlights the role of aristocratic women in preserving and transmitting medical knowledge outside institutional medicine.

[PMID: 42679626] Ictal memory and its clinical correlates in focal epilepsy.

[PMID: ] The vegetative state is not a state of mind

<p>The discovery of covert consciousness (CC) and cognitive motor dissociation (CMD) has revealed a conceptual inconsistency at the heart of disorders of consciousness (DoC) nosology. Advanced neuroimaging and electrophysiological studies have demonstrated that behavioral unresponsiveness does not necessarily imply the absence of consciousness or cognitive capacity, yet contemporary diagnostic categories continue conflating responsiveness with consciousness itself. This conflation is embedded throughout DoC nomenclature, where behavioral syndromes such as the vegetative state (VS) and minimally conscious state (MCS) transform observations of what patients do (or do not do) into inferences about what they experience (i.e., their state of mind). We argue that this category error now impedes the translation of over two decades of scientific advances into a coherent clinical framework. First, it has fostered the widespread but incorrect conflation of covert consciousness (CC) and cognitive motor dissociation (CMD), despite the fact that the two concepts identify different patient populations and may carry distinct implications for diagnosis, prognosis, and treatment. Second, it renders contemporary DoC nosology incapable of coherently incorporating modern neuroimaging and electrophysiological findings. Because the absence of consciousness is an entrenched, definitional component of the vegetative state (VS), its behavioral phenotype cannot be reconciled with covert neurophysiological phenotypes such as CC and CMD. As a result, current nomenclature impedes the development of a phenotype-endotype framework in which bedside behavioral classifications are complemented by biologically informed categories derived from advanced neuroimaging and electrophysiological assessments. We trace the origins of this problem to a historical shift whereby the definitions of vegetative state and minimally conscious state evolved from descriptions of observable behavior into claims about a patient's mental state. Disentangling responsiveness from consciousness, we argue, is essential if the field is to incorporate contemporary evidence and develop a coherent, biologically informed nosology of disorders of consciousness.</p>

[PMID: 42646380] Rehabilitation Outcomes in Patients Who Regained Consciousness.

<b>Background/Objectives</b>: Patients with prolonged disorder of consciousness (PDOC) who regained consciousness in intensive care for consciousness rehabilitation (ICCR) were referred to further brain rehabilitation (FBR). Dynamic Lowenstein Occupational Therapy Cognitive Assessment (DLOTCA) total scores increased from 57 ± 29 to 82 ± 36, and the average 6-min-walk (6MW) distances improved from 295 to 374 m (<i>p</i> < 0.001). The improvement was associated with factors that may mainly reflect the severity of brain damage. The findings support continuing rehabilitation before and after emerging from PDOC.

[PMID: 42512519] Consciousness in the Brain: An Integrative Review of Contemporary Theories.

Understanding consciousness remains one of the most significant challenges in neuroscience, philosophy, and cognitive science. Despite substantial advances in neuroimaging, electrophysiology, and computational modeling, a comprehensive account of the neural basis of conscious experience has yet to emerge. This review examines several leading contemporary theories of consciousness, including the Global Neuronal Workspace Theory (GNWT), Integrated Information Theory (IIT), Recurrent Processing Theory (RPT), Dendritic Integration Theory (DIT), Predictive Processing (PP), and the Memory Theory of Consciousness (MToC). The philosophical foundations, core theoretical claims, and empirical evidence supporting each framework are critically evaluated. Particular attention is given to how these theories address phenomenal consciousness-the subjective quality of experience-and access consciousness, i.e., the availability of information for cognitive control, report, and behavior. By comparing convergent and divergent predictions across theoretical perspectives, this review highlights key areas of agreement, ongoing debates, and unresolved questions in the field. The analysis suggests that consciousness is likely to involve multiple interacting neural mechanisms operating across different spatial and temporal scales, underscoring the need for continued interdisciplinary research to advance a more comprehensive understanding of conscious experience.

After applying exclusion criteria, a final cohort of 119 patients (77 males and 42 females) was included for analysis. Within 1 week of enrollment, the Chinese Coma Recovery Scale-Revised (CRS-R) score and serum sex hormone levels were assessed. Outcomes were evaluated at 6 months using the Glasgow Outcome Scale-Extended (GOS-E) and classified as good outcome(GOSE score≥4) or poor outcome(GOSE score <4). However, these findings did not survive correction for multiple testing, and the predictive performance was modest. Sex hormone levels did not demonstrate clear prognostic value in female patients. These preliminary observations underscore the need for larger, prospective studies with standardized endocrine protocols to validate the potential role of sex hormones as prognostic biomarkers in pDoC.

[PMID: 42909241] Deep brain stimulation and spinal cord stimulation for chronic disorders of consciousness: a decade of clinical evidence, mechanistic insights, and critical appraisal.

Deep brain stimulation (DBS) and spinal cord stimulation (SCS) have been investigated for over four decades as neuromodulatory treatments for chronic disorders of consciousness (DOC), including the vegetative state/unresponsive wakefulness syndrome (VS/UWS) and the minimally conscious state (MCS), for which no disease-modifying pharmacological therapy exists. This narrative review synthesizes literature published between 2016 and 2026 on the neurobiological rationale, clinical evidence, neurophysiological biomarkers, and safety of DBS and SCS for chronic DOC. The 'mesocircuit hypothesis', supported by human intraoperative electrophysiology and nonhuman primate causal manipulation, provides a coherent rationale for central thalamic and centromedian-parafascicular targeting. Across case series, cohort studies, and individual patient data meta-analyses, both DBS and SCS produce clinically meaningful improvement in a minority of patients, consistently more often in MCS (63-74% response) than in VS/UWS (26-30% response), with younger age and shorter injury-to-treatment interval predicting better outcomes. However, the two largest individual patient data meta-analyses of DBS concur that current evidence is insufficient to conclude that stimulation alters the natural history of neurological recovery beyond what would be expected from patient selection and spontaneous recovery, and adverse event rates (8.1% for SCS, 18.2% for DBS) indicate non-trivial surgical risk in this vulnerable population. Neurophysiological and neuroimaging biomarkers derived from EEG, functional near-infrared spectroscopy, functional MRI, and FDG-PET show promise for objective outcome prediction but await prospective validation. We conclude that DBS and SCS remain experimental, mechanistically motivated but not yet definitively proven therapies for chronic DOC, and that adequately powered randomized controlled trials, biomarker-guided patient selection, and dedicated ethical frameworks are needed before either modality can be recommended outside of research settings.

[PMID: ] "Unmasking Misinformation": Characterizing the Social Media Landscape of Disorders of Consciousness on X (formerly Twitter)

<title>Abstract</title>  <p>Objective  To characterize the discursive landscape of public discussion related to disorders of consciousness (DoC) on the X social media platform, including its nature, prevalence, thematic structure, and contextual diversity. Methods  Point-prevalence, observational study of social media content using a combined social network analysis and unsupervised topic-modeling approach. The setting was X (formerly Twitter), a global public microblogging platform. English-language posts published between January 1 and December 31, 2023 were retrieved using a curated set of nine DoC-related keyword phrases ("Disorders of Consciousness," "Coma Recovery," "Vegetative State," "Minimally Conscious State," "Brain Death," "Locked-In Syndrome," "Medically Induced Coma," "Coma Prognosis," "Dead Zone"). No interventions were applied. Results  A total of 30,621 posts were retrieved. BERTopic transformer-based topic modeling yielded 50 coherent topics, which were grouped through structured thematic synthesis into three dominant discourse domains: (1) medical and clinical (vegetative state, brain death, locked-in syndrome, medically induced coma, and prominent named cases); (2) ethical, legal, and societal (organ donation/procurement, end-of-life and beginning-of-life debates, criminal justice cases involving vegetative outcomes); and (3) figurative and non-medical (the term "dead zone" used in technology, gaming, popular culture, and Stephen King's novel). Non-medical uses constituted a substantial fraction of total retrieval volume, and the unsupervised model was unable to algorithmically separate clinical from figurative uses of overlapping terms. Conclusions  Public discourse about DoC on X is substantial in volume, thematically heterogeneous, and embedded within emotionally charged, legally complex, and figurative contexts that dilute clinical meaning and create fertile conditions for misinformation. These findings provide an evidence-based map of where targeted educational and public-health interventions are most needed and underscore the value of cross-platform, multilingual, and domain-specific NLP follow-up work.</p>

[PMID: ] A multimodal investigation of altered cortical metabolism in patients with disorders of consciousness

Disorders of consciousness (DoC) are characterised by marked reductions in cerebral glucose metabolism, but the specific cellular and neurochemical processes driving this impairment remain unclear. To elucidate these mechanisms, a multimodal imaging approach combining [  18 F]-fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography (FDG-PET) and magnetic resonance spectroscopy (MRS) was applied to the posterior medial cortex (PMC) in 15 DoC patients and 14 healthy controls. Regional FDG metabolic index (FDG-MI) values and MRS concentration estimates of glutamate plus glutamine (Glx), γ-aminobutyric acid (GABA+), total N-acetylaspartate (tNAA), total creatine (tCr), and total choline (tCho) were compared between groups and evaluated against Coma Recovery Scale–Revised (CRS-R) scores at baseline and follow-up (mean 127 days). Furthermore, baseline tCho concentrations positively predicted long-term clinical recovery, as indexed by changes in CRS-R scores at follow-up. These findings suggest that macroscopic glucose hypometabolism in DoC may be linked to disrupted intracellular mitochondrial energy production pathways and a shift towards relative synaptic inhibition in the PMC. Moreover, the association between choline levels and behavioural recovery may point to the relevance of retained structural synthesis capacity for functional outcomes.

[PMID: 42848638] Glasgow Coma Scale in Brazilian Portuguese: foundation translation and cultural adaptation study.

<h4>Objective</h4>To establish the foundation for the translation and culturally adaptation of the Glasgow Coma Scale into Brazilian Portuguese as part of a larger project translating and validating another tool, the SCAT-5, of which the Glasgow Coma Scale is a component. The Glasgow Coma Scale assesses the level of consciousness and neurological prognosis after traumatic brain injury. However, a systematic translation and cultural adaptation into Portuguese has never been performed.<h4>Methods</h4>Three Brazilian individuals independently translated the original Glasgow Coma Scale from English to Portuguese. The Glasgow Coma Scale is divided into three separate modules: eye opening (scored between 1 and 4), verbal response (scored between 1 and 5), and best motor response (scored between 1 and 6). The total score is calculated by adding up the scores for each individual module, so it ranges between 3 and 15. Each sentence or expression within the original Glasgow Coma Scale questionnaire was evaluated individually. A multi-professional committee voted and discussed the best translation for each sentence or expression and combined them into a single version, which was back-translated into English by two separate native English professionals and compared with the original to resolve semantic discrepancies. The resulting combined and optimized version was tested twice in a fortnight and applied to 36 athletes who had suffered brain trauma, with cerebral concussion, and were treated in a sports-specialized ambulatory. This test was conducted to evaluate mainly the comprehension of the physician applying the translated questionnaire.<h4>Results</h4>Patients were aged between 18 and 45 years, mostly rugby players (23/36), and female (23/36). There was no variability in the results between patients or between the same patient in both evaluations; all patients were consistently evaluated as having a Glasgow score of 15, since all patients were outpatients.<h4>Conclusion</h4>This study laid the foundations for the translation and cultural adaptation of the Glasgow Coma Scale for Brazilian physicians. This translation may contribute to facilitating its use and standardizing the healthcare of patients with traumatic brain injury in Brazil. Further studies may be necessary to evaluate the use of the translated version in patients with lower levels of consciousness (i.e., lower Glasgow Coma Scale scores).
